# Chapter 5: Water Scarcity and Drought

*Theme: water scarcity and drought*

This chapter examines water scarcity and drought. It moves from mechanism to evidence to what can be done, and each section states its main claim before the supporting material. Where the evidence is uncertain, the text says so; where it is strong, the language is correspondingly direct.

## 5.1 Global Water Cycle

Peter Gleick (2020) puts it plainly: water crises are governance crises magnified by climate, not just hydrology.

Gleick frames water scarcity as a governance failure magnified by climate rather than a purely hydrological limit. Allocation rules, pricing and infrastructure decide who is short. Drought turns a distribution problem into a crisis. Read against global water cycle, the point is that intention without capacity changes little.

The distribution of harm is not a detail appended to the analysis; it is the analysis. Two households can face the same event and emerge with different futures, because what they own, owe and can claim determines recovery (IPCC, 2022). The aggregate picture obscures this: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). For that reason, average-based targets can be met while the most exposed end up worse off. For water utilities and irrigated agriculture, the measures that matter are those judged by their effect on the bottom of the distribution, not the middle. That is also the test of whether a policy is serious.

Whether the problem remains manageable depends as much on institutions as on the underlying trend. Early warning, land-use rules, standards and social protection reduce harm, but only where authority and funding reach the level at which risk is felt (UNDRR, 2015). The common failure is fragmentation: overlapping mandates, unshared data, responsibility falling between agencies. Coordination is a design problem with measurable features — joint protocols, common baselines, clear triggers. Within water utilities and irrigated agriculture, the test is whether its own arrangements have those features before the next event rather than after it. Where such arrangements exist, response times fall and losses follow.

The case for spending early rests on a comparison that is rarely made explicit: prevention against recovery, over decades rather than one budget cycle. Damage functions and observed recovery costs point the same way (World Bank, 2022). The order of magnitude is contested in detail but not in direction: ~2 billion people lack safe water (WHO & UNICEF, 2023). Prevention loses politically because its benefits are invisible: the disaster that did not happen has no constituency. As far as water utilities and irrigated agriculture is concerned, the fix is institutional — budget rules that reserve funds for prevention, and appraisal that counts avoided losses. Otherwise the arithmetic is sound and the incentives are not.

The next decade will show whether preparation or improvisation prevails. Three signals are worth tracking: how often events exceed design thresholds, how much public spending goes to prevention rather than repair, and whether the most exposed groups gain access before a disaster rather than after it (IPCC, 2023). The baseline is already shifting: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). None of those signals is a perfect measure, but together they indicate direction. In the work of water utilities and irrigated agriculture, adaptation is not a state to be reached but a standing capacity to revise decisions as evidence changes. The test is whether the next surprise is met with a plan or with a scramble.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: California's atmospheric rivers in winter 2022-23 ended drought but caused $4.6B flood damages (NOAA, CA DWR). What the report adds is timing, not a new mechanism. As far as water utilities and irrigated agriculture is concerned, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

Two cautions apply to everything in this section. First, most figures are aggregates, and aggregates travel badly between countries and neighbourhoods. Second, published estimates lag the climate they describe. Both cautions argue for revisiting the numbers rather than treating them as fixed. For water utilities and irrigated agriculture, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 5.2 Drought Types and Indices

As Raj Patel (2022) argues, hunger reflects power, not scarcity — climate shocks deepen those power gaps.

Patel argues that hunger reflects power and access rather than global scarcity. Climate shocks deepen existing gaps in entitlement. The framing shifts attention from production to distribution. The implication for drought types and indices is a question of sequence: what has to be in place before the event, not after it.

Institutional performance in drought types and indices depends less on formal authority than on whether mandates, money and information arrive together. Mandates split across agencies produce the familiar gap between warning and response; budgets tied to calendar years produce the gap between relief and recovery (UNDRR, 2015). Neither gap requires bad faith, only an ordinary division of labour. The strain is already visible in the numbers: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). Within water utilities and irrigated agriculture, the design question is where to place the authority that can move money and people within the same week. Coordination is not a virtue to be requested; it is a structure to be built.

Money spent before an event buys more protection than money spent after it. That claim rests on damage functions and on the repeated observation that recovery costs exceed prevention costs by a wide margin (World Bank, 2022). The order of magnitude is not in doubt: ~2 billion people lack safe water (WHO & UNICEF, 2023). What is uncertain is how benefits are distributed and how quickly they appear, which is why appraisal methods matter. As far as water utilities and irrigated agriculture is concerned, the relevant question is not whether prevention pays but who captures the return. Financing arrangements that ignore that question tend to fund visible damage and neglect invisible capacity.

Anyone deciding about drought types and indices is choosing under conditions that will not improve before the choice has to be made. That makes sequencing the central skill: measures that are cheap, reversible and useful under several futures go first, and irreversible commitments wait for better information (IPCC, 2022). The sequence matters because early options buy the time that later options need. The pressure is documented rather than projected: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). In the work of water utilities and irrigated agriculture, the test of a plan is not whether it is optimal for one forecast but whether it survives several. Decisions taken now will be judged by events that were not in the forecast.

The burden falls first on people with the least room to manoeuvre. Those with insecure tenure, irregular income or caring responsibilities have less capacity to move, insure or wait for compensation (IPCC, 2022). The scale is not marginal: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). Because disadvantages compound, the same event can be an inconvenience for one family and a permanent loss for another. For water utilities and irrigated agriculture, equity is therefore a design criterion rather than a footnote. Measures that reach the most exposed first tend to perform better for everyone else as well.

Current reporting adds detail rather than a new direction: Rhine River fell to critical lows in Aug 2022, disrupting 80% of German inland shipping (German Federal Waterways, Reuters). The event is best read as a measurement, not as a verdict. In the work of water utilities and irrigated agriculture, what matters is not the record but the lead time it implies.

The argument, reduced to its shortest form, is this: the physical signal is robust, the human exposure is uneven, and the response is a choice. Each element rests on evidence of different strength, and the weakest link is not the science but the capacity to act before the event rather than after it. Within water utilities and irrigated agriculture, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 5.3 Causes of Water Scarcity

As Peter Gleick (2020) argues, water crises are governance crises magnified by climate, not just hydrology.

Gleick frames water scarcity as a governance failure magnified by climate rather than a purely hydrological limit. Allocation rules, pricing and infrastructure decide who is short. Drought turns a distribution problem into a crisis. In the terms of causes of water scarcity, the claim shifts attention from prediction to preparation.

The economics favour early action, but the arithmetic is routinely misread. Damage functions show rising marginal losses with warming, while preventive spending delivers returns over decades (World Bank, 2022). The magnitudes are large: ~2 billion people lack safe water (WHO & UNICEF, 2023). The obstacle is less the total than the distribution of costs and benefits: those who pay are rarely those who are protected. As far as water utilities and irrigated agriculture is concerned, the implication is to count avoided losses, not only capital outlays. Insurance can spread the residual risk; it cannot reduce the hazard itself.

The relevant question for causes of water scarcity is not what will happen but what to do while the answer is still forming. That favours measures that are cheap, reversible and useful across several futures. Early warning, insurance, redundant systems and protected water sources meet that test; megaprojects tied to one scenario do not (IPCC, 2023). Pressure is building regardless: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). In the work of water utilities and irrigated agriculture, the portfolio matters more than any single measure. Robustness, not optimality, is the right criterion under uncertainty.

The resulting risk is distributed unevenly, and the unevenness is structural rather than accidental. Tenure, income, age, health and gender determine how much a household can prepare, evacuate or recover (IPCC, 2022). The aggregate numbers conceal this: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). Averages describe the size of the problem, not who carries it. Decisions taken by water utilities and irrigated agriculture — where to build, whom to insure, which services keep running — widen that gap or close it. A policy that protects the average household while leaving the most exposed unprotected has not solved the problem it claims to solve. For water utilities and irrigated agriculture, the practical question is what would have to change first.

Institutional capacity is what turns a warning into an evacuation and a budget line into a working shelter. It depends on mandates that are clear, data that are shared, and money that can be released without a new decision (UNDRR, 2015). Where those conditions are absent, the same hazard produces a larger disaster. The stakes are visible in the numbers: ~2 billion people lack safe water (WHO & UNICEF, 2023). Within water utilities and irrigated agriculture, capability is better tested in a drill than in a document. A plan that has never been exercised is a hypothesis.

A recent marker points the same way: California's atmospheric rivers in winter 2022-23 ended drought but caused $4.6B flood damages (NOAA, CA DWR). Reported in isolation the figure looks dramatic; reported against the baseline it looks consistent. For water utilities and irrigated agriculture, the item is useful mainly as a date stamp on a trend.

Two limits apply. The first is scale: global figures say little about a district, and district figures are often unavailable. The second is time: the record is long enough to detect a trend but not long enough to rule out rare extremes. Good planning treats both as design constraints rather than reasons to postpone. As far as water utilities and irrigated agriculture is concerned, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 5.4 Agricultural Water Use

Raj Patel (2022) puts it plainly: hunger reflects power, not scarcity — climate shocks deepen those power gaps.

Patel argues that hunger reflects power and access rather than global scarcity. Climate shocks deepen existing gaps in entitlement. The framing shifts attention from production to distribution. For agricultural water use, the practical consequence is to name the decision that would have to change.

Any assessment of agricultural water use will turn less on what is promised now than on what is in place when the next event arrives. Plans that exist only on paper, thresholds that trigger no action, and funds that cannot be released quickly are the recurring failure modes (UNDRR, 2015). The pressure is not hypothetical: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). Progress can be measured in lead times, in the share of exposed households covered by warning and insurance, and in how quickly services return. In the work of water utilities and irrigated agriculture, reviewing those measures on a fixed cycle works better than reviewing them after each disaster. Iteration is the difference between adaptation and repeated recovery.

The aggregate picture hides the distribution, and in agricultural water use the distribution carries most of the policy content. Risk is concentrated by geography, tenure and income, and the concentration is produced by prior decisions about land, services and insurance (UNDRR, 2015). Because those decisions were made deliberately, they can be unmade deliberately. The unevenness is measurable: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). For water utilities and irrigated agriculture, averages are the wrong unit of account; the relevant figure is the burden carried by the group least able to carry it. A policy judged by its average effect will systematically miss the people it is meant to protect.

Governance is tested at the seams between organisations. Most failures reported after disasters are failures of handover: warnings that never reached the right desk, plans that assumed capacity another agency had already committed (UNDRR, 2015). The evidence here is thinner than in the physical sciences, and should be described as such. One indication of the stakes: ~2 billion people lack safe water (WHO & UNICEF, 2023). What the record does show is that authority, money and information have to move together; any one without the others stalls. Within water utilities and irrigated agriculture, the test is whether a decision can be taken where the consequence is felt, with the resources to act on it.

The economics of agricultural water use are usually presented as a bill, which is the least useful form in which to present them. Costs arrive early and are budgeted, while benefits arrive late and are unassigned; the asymmetry systematically biases public investment toward response and away from preparation (World Bank, 2023). Insurance and credit then transmit that bias into private decisions about where to build and what to protect. The magnitude is not marginal: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). As far as water utilities and irrigated agriculture is concerned, the useful accounting separates the cost of preparing from the cost of not preparing, and prices both over the same horizon. A measure that is expensive against one budget year is often cheap against ten.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Rhine River fell to critical lows in Aug 2022, disrupting 80% of German inland shipping (German Federal Waterways, Reuters). On its own it proves little; placed against the trend it shows the direction of travel. Within water utilities and irrigated agriculture, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

None of this settles the political question of how much to spend and who should pay. It does settle a prior question: whether there is anything worth spending on. The evidence says there is, and that the returns depend on acting while the options are still open. In the work of water utilities and irrigated agriculture, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 5.5 Case Study: Water Scarcity and Drought

A single episode illustrates the mechanism: California's atmospheric rivers in winter 2022-23 ended drought but caused $4.6B flood damages (NOAA, CA DWR). Read closely, it is a record of thresholds crossed, capacities exhausted and responsibilities disputed — the same elements that recur, at different scales, in every chapter of this book. Within water utilities and irrigated agriculture, it is the clearest recent test of whether the arrangements on paper survive contact with an event.

The instructive part is the asymmetry between the event and its aftermath. Response times are measured in hours, recovery in years, and the difference between the two is usually decided before the event — by land use, insurance and local authority, all of which water utilities and irrigated agriculture controls.

The test of any lesson drawn from the case is whether it changes a budget line, a standard or a protocol. If it changes none of the three, it remains a story.



## 5.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Water Scarcity and Drought

For water scarcity and drought, the near term shows up in wells and reservoirs: by 2040 renewable water per person falls 25–35% in Java, northern India and the Sahel, groundwater drops 0.5–1.5 m a year, and reservoirs deliver 30% less in dry seasons.

By 2050, drier soils and shorter rainy seasons push 1.5–2.5 billion people into water stress, and by 2100 a high-emissions path cuts river flows 30–50% and dries 40% of shallow wells (IPCC, 2022; WHO & UNICEF, 2023).

Rainwater harvesting on every roof, drip irrigation that cuts use 30–50%, managed aquifer recharge, and village water banks with clear rationing rules buy time.

[[TABLE:Table 5.1 Future scenarios and predictions for water scarcity and drought (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | renewable water −15–25% |
| 2050 | 1,5–2,5 billion in water stress |
| 2100 | river flows −30–50%; 40% wells dry |

Watch dry-season reservoir levels, the depth to groundwater in your nearest monitoring well, and the number of tanker deliveries; rainfall trends are medium-confidence, but groundwater decline is directly measurable.

# Chapter 6: Floods and Sea Level Rise

*Theme: floods and sea level rise*

The subject of this chapter is floods and sea level rise. The argument proceeds in three steps: what is happening, how we know, and what follows for decisions. Sources are cited as they are used, and contested points are marked as contested rather than presented as settled.

## 6.1 Flood Types and Causes

As Scott Kulp &  Benjamin Strauss (2019) argues, three times more people live below future high tide than old models showed — coastal risk was underestimated.

Kulp and Strauss corrected coastal elevation data using radar and found that far more land and people sit below future high-tide lines than earlier models showed. Their estimates made exposure visible at the scale of individual settlements. The projections assume stated emissions pathways. For flood types and causes, the bearing is direct: the constraint is institutional as much as physical.

Institutional performance in flood types and causes depends less on formal authority than on whether mandates, money and information arrive together. Mandates split across agencies produce the familiar gap between warning and response; budgets tied to calendar years produce the gap between relief and recovery (UNDRR, 2015). Neither gap requires bad faith, only an ordinary division of labour. The strain is already visible in the numbers: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). In the work of coastal planning and flood defence, the design question is where to place the authority that can move money and people within the same week. Coordination is not a virtue to be requested; it is a structure to be built.

Most disagreements about flood types and causes are not disagreements about facts. They are disagreements about weight: which risks count, over what horizon, and for whom (IPCC, 2022). That is why the same evidence can support both alarm and complacency without either side misreading the data. The scale involved is not marginal: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). For coastal planning and flood defence, clarity about the weighting matters as much as clarity about the measurement. Stating the criterion in advance is what separates analysis from advocacy.

The evidence base behind flood types and causes is larger than its public profile suggests, and more uneven. Satellite records, station data, insurance claims and household surveys answer different questions, and they rarely agree on the same decimal place (IPCC, 2021). Where they disagree, the disagreement is usually about coverage rather than about the direction of change. Confidence is highest on aggregates and lowest on localities: sea level has risen ~20–25 cm since 1900 (IPCC, 2021). Within coastal planning and flood defence, the sensible use of the record is to establish direction and then test local plausibility rather than to import a global average wholesale. A number that cannot be traced to a source is not evidence; it is decoration.

Why things behave as they do is a question with a traceable answer. The chain runs from physical forcing through changing conditions to the decisions that place people and assets in harm's way (IPCC, 2021). Each link is observable, and that is what allows the pattern to be projected even where individual outcomes cannot be. The magnitudes involved are not small: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). As far as coastal planning and flood defence is concerned, there is more than one point of action, and the cheapest interventions are usually the earliest. Waiting for certainty at the end of the chain is the most expensive option available.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Typhoon Doksuri flooded Beijing in July 2023 with heaviest rain in 140 years, 1 million displaced (China CMA, Reuters). It is one line in a longer ledger, and the ledger is what matters. In the work of coastal planning and flood defence, what matters is not the record but the lead time it implies.

Certainty is unevenly distributed across this literature. Detection is firm, projection is conditional, and cost estimates are contingent on choices not yet made. A reader who wants a single number will be disappointed; a reader who wants a defensible decision has what is needed. In the work of coastal planning and flood defence, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 6.2 Sea Level Rise Mechanisms

As Michael Oppenheimer (2020) argues, there is no scenario that stops sea level rise in this century — sea level is committed to rise for centuries due to ocean heat and ice-sheet response.

Oppenheimer's statement draws on his role as Lead Author of IPCC SROCC Chapter 4 (2019) on sea-level rise and coastal implications. 'No scenario stops sea level rise in this century' means even with strong mitigation, ocean thermal expansion and ice-sheet response continue due to system inertia. 'Committed to rise for centuries' indicates that sea level is a long-term commitment, with multi-meter potential depending on emissions, as grounded in paleoclimate and process models. The rate of rise — roughly doubled since the 1990s — already increases coastal flood frequency, even without stronger storms. Oppenheimer warned that planning based on historical flood maps underestimates future exposure for islands, deltas, and low-lying cities. For sea level rise mechanisms within floods and sea level rise, the analysis must shift from preventing rise to managing inevitable rise through elevation, protection, accommodation, and managed retreat, with timelines spanning decades to centuries. Read against sea level rise mechanisms, the point is that intention without capacity changes little.

Discussion of sea level rise mechanisms usually begins as a technical question, and that framing hides the decisions underneath it. The technical part is real: measurement, modelling and attribution have improved sharply over two decades (IPCC, 2023). But the contested part is rarely the measurement; it is what the numbers imply for who acts, who pays and who is protected. One figure sets the scale: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). Read that way, the question for coastal planning and flood defence is not whether the trend is real but which choices remain defensible across the plausible range. A framing that treats uncertainty as a reason to wait gets the logic backwards: uncertainty is what makes early, reversible decisions valuable. For coastal planning and flood defence, that is the difference between a plan that can be defended and one that merely exists.

The evidence base for sea level rise mechanisms is broad but uneven, and the unevenness is informative. Where many independent studies agree, confidence is high; where a single model family supplies the estimate, it is not (IPCC, 2021). One figure illustrates the state of knowledge: sea level has risen ~20–25 cm since 1900 (IPCC, 2021). The appropriate response to a strong signal with weak local detail is not to wait for precision but to design for the range. Within coastal planning and flood defence, that means carrying uncertainty into the decision rather than resolving it beforehand. Plans that require certainty before acting are plans that act late.

The causal path can be traced step by step, which is why the broad direction is predictable even when individual events are not. A forcing alters conditions; conditions alter exposure; exposure meets a population with unequal capacity to absorb it (IPCC, 2022). Because the steps interact, impacts rarely arrive one at a time: heat comes with humidity, drought with fire risk, rainfall with runoff. The same pattern is visible in the numbers: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). As far as coastal planning and flood defence is concerned, the practical implication is to plan for combinations rather than single hazards. Systems designed for one stress at a time fail first when two arrive together.

Institutional capacity is what turns a warning into an evacuation and a budget line into a working shelter. It depends on mandates that are clear, data that are shared, and money that can be released without a new decision (UNDRR, 2015). Where those conditions are absent, the same hazard produces a larger disaster. The stakes are visible in the numbers: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). In the work of coastal planning and flood defence, capability is better tested in a drill than in a document. A plan that has never been exercised is a hypothesis.

Current reporting adds detail rather than a new direction: Peru's Cyclone Yaku (Mar 2023) brought unprecedented rains to arid north, 70 deaths (SENAMHI, Reuters). Set beside the record, the episode indicates direction without fixing the rate. For coastal planning and flood defence, the item is useful mainly as a date stamp on a trend.

What emerges is less a prediction than a test. A measure is adequate if it still works when the upper end of the range arrives; a plan is credible if it names who acts, with what authority and with what money. Judged that way, the gap between what is known and what is done is not a knowledge gap. For coastal planning and flood defence, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 6.3 Coastal Flood Risk

W. Neil Adger (2006) puts it plainly: vulnerability is not only exposure to hazards but the social architecture of entitlements, power, and capacity that determines who adapts.

Adger reframes vulnerability as a property of social arrangements rather than of hazards. Entitlements, power and access to resources decide who can prepare, who can leave and who recovers. The point is structural, not semantic. The implication for coastal flood risk is a question of sequence: what has to be in place before the event, not after it.

The record on coastal flood risk is now strong enough to be treated as a trend rather than an anomaly. The record is uneven — some regions and seasons move sharply, others barely — but the aggregate direction is consistent across methods (IPCC, 2023). The headline figure is blunt: sea level has risen ~20–25 cm since 1900 (IPCC, 2021). What remains contested is attribution of individual events and the speed of future change, not whether the baseline has shifted. That distinction shapes how coastal planning and flood defence should read the numbers: as a distribution to plan against, not a forecast to await. Where the evidence is thin, the honest response is a stated limitation rather than a confident sentence. Within coastal planning and flood defence, the test is whether the arrangement survives contact with a bad week.

Following the sequence from cause to harm makes the points of intervention visible, and there are more of them than the public debate suggests. Emissions determine the forcing; land use and insurance determine exposure; income, health and warning determine capacity; each link can be altered by policy at a different scale (UNDRR, 2015). Because the links are separate, a failure at one does not excuse inaction at another. The scale at which the chain operates is large: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). As far as coastal planning and flood defence is concerned, the practical value of thinking in a chain is that it shows which link is cheapest to move this year. Intervention points are political choices, not physical facts.

Governance is tested at the seams between organisations. Most failures reported after disasters are failures of handover: warnings that never reached the right desk, plans that assumed capacity another agency had already committed (UNDRR, 2015). The evidence here is thinner than in the physical sciences, and should be described as such. One indication of the stakes: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). What the record does show is that authority, money and information have to move together; any one without the others stalls. In the work of coastal planning and flood defence, the test is whether a decision can be taken where the consequence is felt, with the resources to act on it.

How a problem is framed decides what counts as a solution, and coastal flood risk is a case in point. Framed as an environmental issue, it invites a ministry and a budget line; framed as a development issue, it invites land use, housing and finance at once (IPCC, 2022). The second framing is harder to administer precisely because it cannot be assigned to a single department. The scale involved rules out a narrow frame: sea level has risen ~20–25 cm since 1900 (IPCC, 2021). For coastal planning and flood defence, the practical consequence is that the frame has to be chosen before the plan, not after it. Frames are cheap to change on paper and expensive to change in an institution.

A recent marker points the same way: Typhoon Doksuri flooded Beijing in July 2023 with heaviest rain in 140 years, 1 million displaced (China CMA, Reuters). Read against the longer record, it is an illustration rather than a proof. Within coastal planning and flood defence, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

Readers should know where this section stands on the evidence ladder. The direction of change is established; the pace is estimated; the local consequences are modelled. Statements that blur those levels are the ones most likely to mislead, and they are also the easiest to check. Within coastal planning and flood defence, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 6.4 Riverine and Flash Floods

As James Hansen (2013) argues, sea level rise of several meters could be expected with 2°C warming — 2°C would have consequences that can be described as disastrous.

Hansen's statement originates from his 2016 paper 'Ice melt, sea level rise and superstorms' and a 2013 op-ed that warned about the long-term consequences of 2°C warming. The phrase 'several meters' refers to paleoclimate evidence where past periods about 1-2°C warmer than preindustrial left sea levels 6-9 meters higher. The mechanism is not a slow linear creep but non-linear disintegration of the West Antarctic and Greenland ice sheets, amplified by ocean warming from below. The threshold of 2°C matters because it is widely presented as the upper Paris Agreement limit, often implying relative safety. Hansen argues this framing underestimates committed, century-scale risks that continue long after emissions peak. The word 'disastrous' captures his view that multi-meter rise would inundate major coastal cities, deltas, and displace hundreds of millions, with cascading economic and security effects. For riverine and flash floods within floods and sea level rise, the implication is that near-term temperature targets must be judged against long-term, irreversible sea-level commitments, not just mid-century warming levels. In the terms of riverine and flash floods, the claim shifts attention from prediction to preparation.

Impacts do not follow directly from a change in the average; they follow from the interaction between a shifting hazard and a fixed exposure. A house built to a hundred-year flood standard meets a twenty-five-year event with alarming regularity once the distribution has moved (IPCC, 2022). Nothing about the house changed; the standard it was designed against did. The shift is quantifiable: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). As far as coastal planning and flood defence is concerned, the lesson is that design standards have a shelf life and need a review date attached to them. An unrevised standard is a decision to accept a higher level of risk than the one originally debated.

Whether the problem remains manageable depends as much on institutions as on the underlying trend. Early warning, land-use rules, standards and social protection reduce harm, but only where authority and funding reach the level at which risk is felt (UNDRR, 2015). The common failure is fragmentation: overlapping mandates, unshared data, responsibility falling between agencies. Coordination is a design problem with measurable features — joint protocols, common baselines, clear triggers. In the work of coastal planning and flood defence, the test is whether its own arrangements have those features before the next event rather than after it. Where such arrangements exist, response times fall and losses follow.

It helps to ask what question riverine and flash floods is really answering. Framed as a measurement problem, it invites better instruments; framed as a decision problem, it invites better institutions (IPCC, 2023). The difference is practical, because the second framing admits trade-offs that the first hides. The scale is already visible: sea level has risen ~20–25 cm since 1900 (IPCC, 2021). For coastal planning and flood defence, the choice of framing determines which options are considered at all. A question that excludes trade-offs will produce an answer that cannot be implemented.

Evidence on riverine and flash floods rests on several independent lines rather than a single study. Satellites, ground stations, ocean measurements and model reanalyses now agree on direction, and disagree mainly on pace and regional detail (IPCC, 2021). One measure puts the magnitude in view: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Confidence is highest where those lines converge and lowest where one method or a short record carries the claim. Within coastal planning and flood defence, that means planning against a range rather than a point. A decision built on the central estimate alone is a decision that will be surprised.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Peru's Cyclone Yaku (Mar 2023) brought unprecedented rains to arid north, 70 deaths (SENAMHI, Reuters). The value of such reports is that they date the change rather than explain it. As far as coastal planning and flood defence is concerned, what matters is not the record but the lead time it implies.

What the section establishes is narrower than a prediction and firmer than an opinion. It establishes that the direction is known, that the costs of inaction are concentrated on those least able to bear them, and that the available measures are known well enough to be tested. As far as coastal planning and flood defence is concerned, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 6.5 Case Study: Floods and Sea Level Rise

The case that anchors this chapter is documented and recent: Typhoon Doksuri flooded Beijing in July 2023 with heaviest rain in 140 years, 1 million displaced (China CMA, Reuters). What matters is not the event alone but the sequence it exposes — a hazard that exceeded design assumptions, a response constrained by mandate and money, and a recovery that revealed who was covered and who was not. As far as coastal planning and flood defence is concerned, the record supplies both the warning and the timetable.

Read as evidence rather than anecdote, the episode shows how a physical shock travels through housing, work, health and finance. Damage does not stop where the water recedes; it continues through lost income, interrupted schooling and depleted savings — a sequence coastal planning and flood defence can measure in its own records.

The test of any lesson drawn from the case is whether it changes a budget line, a standard or a protocol. If it changes none of the three, it remains a story.



## 6.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Floods and Sea Level Rise

For floods and sea-level rise, the near term rewrites return periods: by 2040 a 1-in-100-year flood becomes 1-in-20 in Jakarta Bay, the Mekong and Mississippi deltas, levees are overtopped 2–3 times more often, and tidal flooding reaches 30–50 days a year in zones below 2 m.

By 2050, 0.2–0.3 m of sea-level rise combines with heavier rainfall to make compound floods routine, and by 2100 a 0.6–1.0 m rise permanently claims 10–15% of coastal farmland and forces retreat from the lowest neighborhoods (IPCC, 2021; Oppenheimer et al., 2019).

Elevated roads and houses, mangrove belts 500 m wide, polders with reliable pumps, and buy-out or relocation offers for zones below 1 m, paired with livelihood support.

[[TABLE:Table 6.1 Future scenarios and predictions for floods and sea level rise (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | 1-in-100 → 1-in-30 flood; +0.10 m sea |
| 2050 | 1-in-100 → 1-in-15; 20–30 tidal days |
| 2100 | 0.6–1.0 m; 10–15% coastal farmland lost |

Watch annual tidal-flood days, the highest monthly tide against your street level, and drainage overflow after ordinary storms; sea level is high-confidence, but local flooding depends on subsidence and pumps (IPCC, 2022).

# Chapter 7: Food Security and Agriculture

*Theme: food security and agriculture under climate change*

This chapter deals with food security and agriculture under climate change. Each section opens with a claim, tests it against the evidence and draws out the implication for the people who have to act on it. The aim is not completeness but a defensible line through a large literature.

## 7.1 Food Systems Overview

As David Lobell (2011) argues, warming since 1980 has already cut wheat and maize yields, masking gains from technology.

Lobell's analysis separated climate effects from technology and management in global crop records. Warming since 1980 reduced wheat and maize yields relative to a counterfactual without it. Adaptation and carbon fertilisation offset part, but not all, of the loss. Applied to food systems overview, the argument sets a test: does the measure still work when capacity is stretched?

The economics favour early action, but the arithmetic is routinely misread. Damage functions show rising marginal losses with warming, while preventive spending delivers returns over decades (World Bank, 2022). The magnitudes are large: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). The obstacle is less the total than the distribution of costs and benefits: those who pay are rarely those who are protected. As far as food systems and rural livelihoods is concerned, the implication is to count avoided losses, not only capital outlays. Insurance can spread the residual risk; it cannot reduce the hazard itself.

An honest account of food systems overview distinguishes three things: what has been measured, what has been modelled, and what has been assumed. Most public confusion comes from collapsing the three into one sentence (IPCC, 2021). The distinction is concrete: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Numbers carry confidence intervals, and those intervals should travel with them into any decision. In the work of food systems and rural livelihoods, the discipline is to write the interval next to the estimate whenever the estimate is used. A disclosed range can be planned against; an undisclosed point estimate cannot.

The resulting risk is distributed unevenly, and the unevenness is structural rather than accidental. Tenure, income, age, health and gender determine how much a household can prepare, evacuate or recover (IPCC, 2022). The aggregate numbers conceal this: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). Averages describe the size of the problem, not who carries it. Decisions taken by food systems and rural livelihoods — where to build, whom to insure, which services keep running — widen that gap or close it. A policy that protects the average household while leaving the most exposed unprotected has not solved the problem it claims to solve. For food systems and rural livelihoods, the practical question is what would have to change first.

The relevant question for food systems overview is not what will happen but what to do while the answer is still forming. That favours measures that are cheap, reversible and useful across several futures. Early warning, insurance, redundant systems and protected water sources meet that test; megaprojects tied to one scenario do not (IPCC, 2023). Pressure is building regardless: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). Within food systems and rural livelihoods, the portfolio matters more than any single measure. Robustness, not optimality, is the right criterion under uncertainty.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Spain's 2023 olive harvest halved after drought+heat, driving global olive oil prices up 112% (FAO, IOC). The event is best read as a measurement, not as a verdict. For food systems and rural livelihoods, the item is useful mainly as a date stamp on a trend.

The evidence is not unanimous on pace and regional detail, and the gaps should be named rather than smoothed over. Where estimates differ, the difference usually traces back to assumptions about emissions, adaptation and measurement. That does not weaken the case for action; it defines the range within which action has to be robust. As far as food systems and rural livelihoods is concerned, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 7.2 Crop Yield Impacts

Cynthia Rosenzweig (2020) puts it plainly: the potential risk of multi-breadbasket failure is increasing — food systems face escalating extremes that demand systemic adaptation.

Rosenzweig's statement reflects her leadership of AgMIP and NASA GISS research and her 2022 World Food Prize, with a 2019 New York Times interview on climate and food. 'Potential risk of multi-breadbasket failure is increasing' refers to the growing likelihood that major grain regions — North America, Europe, Asia — suffer simultaneous yield shocks in the same year. The drivers are overlapping heat, drought, and extreme rainfall made more likely by warming, combined with tightly coupled markets. 'Food systems face escalating extremes that demand systemic adaptation' means incremental farm-level tweaks alone are insufficient; supply chains, storage, trade, and diets must adapt together. Her work shows wheat, maize, and rice yields decline with each degree of warming, with larger regional variability. For crop yield impacts within food security and agriculture under climate change, this frames food and agriculture as systemic risk requiring diversification, resilient varieties, ecosystem-based practices, and early action on storage and distribution. For crop yield impacts, the bearing is direct: the constraint is institutional as much as physical.

Not all statements about crop yield impacts carry the same warrant. Some rest on direct measurement, some on models, some on expert judgement, and the three should not be written in the same voice (IPCC, 2021). The numbers are often firmer than the uses to which they are put: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Misreading usually happens in translation — a global average applied to a locality, a scenario treated as a forecast. In the work of food systems and rural livelihoods, the safeguard is to record the source, period and assumptions behind each figure used. Traceability is not bureaucracy; it is what allows a claim to be checked later.

The aggregate picture hides the distribution, and in crop yield impacts the distribution carries most of the policy content. Risk is concentrated by geography, tenure and income, and the concentration is produced by prior decisions about land, services and insurance (UNDRR, 2015). Because those decisions were made deliberately, they can be unmade deliberately. The unevenness is measurable: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). For food systems and rural livelihoods, averages are the wrong unit of account; the relevant figure is the burden carried by the group least able to carry it. A policy judged by its average effect will systematically miss the people it is meant to protect.

Any assessment of crop yield impacts will turn less on what is promised now than on what is in place when the next event arrives. Plans that exist only on paper, thresholds that trigger no action, and funds that cannot be released quickly are the recurring failure modes (UNDRR, 2015). The pressure is not hypothetical: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). Progress can be measured in lead times, in the share of exposed households covered by warning and insurance, and in how quickly services return. Within food systems and rural livelihoods, reviewing those measures on a fixed cycle works better than reviewing them after each disaster. Iteration is the difference between adaptation and repeated recovery.

The economics of crop yield impacts are usually presented as a bill, which is the least useful form in which to present them. Costs arrive early and are budgeted, while benefits arrive late and are unassigned; the asymmetry systematically biases public investment toward response and away from preparation (World Bank, 2023). Insurance and credit then transmit that bias into private decisions about where to build and what to protect. The magnitude is not marginal: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). As far as food systems and rural livelihoods is concerned, the useful accounting separates the cost of preparing from the cost of not preparing, and prices both over the same horizon. A measure that is expensive against one budget year is often cheap against ten.

Current reporting adds detail rather than a new direction: Spain's 2023 olive harvest halved after drought+heat, driving global olive oil prices up 112% (FAO, IOC). Reported in isolation the figure looks dramatic; reported against the baseline it looks consistent. Within food systems and rural livelihoods, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

The strands point in one direction: the physical signal is established, the human consequences depend on exposure and capacity, and the response depends on decisions taken before the event. That sequence is what makes the problem tractable, and what makes delay expensive. In the work of food systems and rural livelihoods, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 7.3 Heat and Water Stress on Crops

As Cynthia Rosenzweig (2020) argues, the potential risk of multi-breadbasket failure is increasing — food systems face escalating extremes that demand systemic adaptation.

Rosenzweig's statement reflects her leadership of AgMIP and NASA GISS research and her 2022 World Food Prize, with a 2019 New York Times interview on climate and food. 'Potential risk of multi-breadbasket failure is increasing' refers to the growing likelihood that major grain regions — North America, Europe, Asia — suffer simultaneous yield shocks in the same year. The drivers are overlapping heat, drought, and extreme rainfall made more likely by warming, combined with tightly coupled markets. 'Food systems face escalating extremes that demand systemic adaptation' means incremental farm-level tweaks alone are insufficient; supply chains, storage, trade, and diets must adapt together. Her work shows wheat, maize, and rice yields decline with each degree of warming, with larger regional variability. For heat and water stress on crops within food security and agriculture under climate change, this frames food and agriculture as systemic risk requiring diversification, resilient varieties, ecosystem-based practices, and early action on storage and distribution. Read against heat and water stress on crops, the point is that intention without capacity changes little.

The distribution of harm is not a detail appended to the analysis; it is the analysis. Two households can face the same event and emerge with different futures, because what they own, owe and can claim determines recovery (IPCC, 2022). The aggregate picture obscures this: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). For that reason, average-based targets can be met while the most exposed end up worse off. For food systems and rural livelihoods, the measures that matter are those judged by their effect on the bottom of the distribution, not the middle. That is also the test of whether a policy is serious.

The next decade will show whether preparation or improvisation prevails. Three signals are worth tracking: how often events exceed design thresholds, how much public spending goes to prevention rather than repair, and whether the most exposed groups gain access before a disaster rather than after it (IPCC, 2023). The baseline is already shifting: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). None of those signals is a perfect measure, but together they indicate direction. Within food systems and rural livelihoods, adaptation is not a state to be reached but a standing capacity to revise decisions as evidence changes. The test is whether the next surprise is met with a plan or with a scramble.

The case for spending early rests on a comparison that is rarely made explicit: prevention against recovery, over decades rather than one budget cycle. Damage functions and observed recovery costs point the same way (World Bank, 2022). The order of magnitude is contested in detail but not in direction: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Prevention loses politically because its benefits are invisible: the disaster that did not happen has no constituency. As far as food systems and rural livelihoods is concerned, the fix is institutional — budget rules that reserve funds for prevention, and appraisal that counts avoided losses. Otherwise the arithmetic is sound and the incentives are not.

The strength of the claim about heat and water stress on crops varies, and that variation should be visible in the prose. Detection and attribution are well supported; regional projections carry wider bands; estimates of future damage depend on assumptions about emissions and adaptation (IPCC, 2021). A concrete example of the difference: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). A central estimate without its range is not more precise, only less honest. In the work of food systems and rural livelihoods, the discipline is to keep scenarios labelled as scenarios and observations labelled as observations. Where the evidence is thin, the correct response is a stated limitation rather than a confident sentence.

A recent marker points the same way: Spain's 2023 olive harvest halved after drought+heat, driving global olive oil prices up 112% (FAO, IOC). On its own it proves little; placed against the trend it shows the direction of travel. As far as food systems and rural livelihoods is concerned, what matters is not the record but the lead time it implies.

Two cautions apply to everything in this section. First, most figures are aggregates, and aggregates travel badly between countries and neighbourhoods. Second, published estimates lag the climate they describe. Both cautions argue for revisiting the numbers rather than treating them as fixed. For food systems and rural livelihoods, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 7.4 Pests and Diseases

As Tim Wheeler (2013) argues, food shocks travel through prices and nutrition, striking import-dependent poor first.

Wheeler and von Braun trace how food shocks travel through prices, trade and nutrition. Import-dependent poor households absorb the first and deepest losses. Short-term food aid does not prevent the next shock. The implication for pests and diseases is a question of sequence: what has to be in place before the event, not after it.

Anyone deciding about pests and diseases is choosing under conditions that will not improve before the choice has to be made. That makes sequencing the central skill: measures that are cheap, reversible and useful under several futures go first, and irreversible commitments wait for better information (IPCC, 2022). The sequence matters because early options buy the time that later options need. The pressure is documented rather than projected: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). Within food systems and rural livelihoods, the test of a plan is not whether it is optimal for one forecast but whether it survives several. Decisions taken now will be judged by events that were not in the forecast.

Money spent before an event buys more protection than money spent after it. That claim rests on damage functions and on the repeated observation that recovery costs exceed prevention costs by a wide margin (World Bank, 2022). The order of magnitude is not in doubt: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). What is uncertain is how benefits are distributed and how quickly they appear, which is why appraisal methods matter. As far as food systems and rural livelihoods is concerned, the relevant question is not whether prevention pays but who captures the return. Financing arrangements that ignore that question tend to fund visible damage and neglect invisible capacity.

Uncertainty in pests and diseases is not a single quantity, and treating it as one is the most common analytic error in the field. Physical uncertainty, scenario uncertainty and model uncertainty behave differently: the first narrows with data, the second depends on choices, the third on structure (IPCC, 2021). Only the first is reduced by waiting; the other two are reduced by deciding. The range is wide enough to matter for planning: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). In the work of food systems and rural livelihoods, the correct response to the wide part of the range is a decision that performs acceptably across it, not a postponement until it narrows. Plans that require certainty before they begin are plans that begin too late.

The burden falls first on people with the least room to manoeuvre. Those with insecure tenure, irregular income or caring responsibilities have less capacity to move, insure or wait for compensation (IPCC, 2022). The scale is not marginal: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). Because disadvantages compound, the same event can be an inconvenience for one family and a permanent loss for another. For food systems and rural livelihoods, equity is therefore a design criterion rather than a footnote. Measures that reach the most exposed first tend to perform better for everyone else as well.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Spain's 2023 olive harvest halved after drought+heat, driving global olive oil prices up 112% (FAO, IOC). Taken alone the episode is weather; taken with the record it is a data point on a slope. In the work of food systems and rural livelihoods, the item is useful mainly as a date stamp on a trend.

The argument, reduced to its shortest form, is this: the physical signal is robust, the human exposure is uneven, and the response is a choice. Each element rests on evidence of different strength, and the weakest link is not the science but the capacity to act before the event rather than after it. Within food systems and rural livelihoods, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 7.5 Case Study: Food Security and Agriculture

One documented episode makes the argument concrete: Spain's 2023 olive harvest halved after drought+heat, driving global olive oil prices up 112% (FAO, IOC). It repays attention less as an outlier than as a chain of decisions — where the hazard was allowed to concentrate, how the response was organised, and who bore the residual loss. In the work of food systems and rural livelihoods, the sequence is worth reconstructing before the next season rather than after it.

Cases of this kind are useful because they compress several mechanisms into a short period. Exposure, capacity and institutional reach become visible at once, and the gaps between them stop being abstract (UNDRR, 2015). For food systems and rural livelihoods, the episode works as a readiness test rather than a weather story.

The test of any lesson drawn from the case is whether it changes a budget line, a standard or a protocol. If it changes none of the three, it remains a story.



## 7.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Food Security and Agriculture

For food security and agriculture, the near term appears in yields and prices: by 2040 wheat yields fall 8–15% and maize 10–18% in South Asia and the Sahel, rice drops 5–10% from night heat, and tropical fish catch falls 10–20%, pushing 40–60 million more people into hunger.

By 2050, simultaneous breadbasket failures become plausible and food prices swing 20–35% in bad years; by 2100 a high-emissions path cuts staple output 20–30% without adaptation, while irrigation demand rises 30–40% (Zhao et al., 2017; FAO, 2023).

Heat-tolerant seeds, sowing 2–3 weeks earlier, drip and mulch saving 30% of water, fish ponds and village grain banks smooth the lean months.

[[TABLE:Table 7.1 Future scenarios and predictions for food security and agriculture under climate change (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | wheat −5%; maize −8% in hot zones |
| 2050 | price swings 20–35%; 40–60 m hungry |
| 2100 | staples −20–30% without adaptation |

Watch local yields per hectare, the date of the first reliable rain, and staple prices in the nearest market; crop response to heat is high-confidence, but farm-level losses depend on soil, seed choice and irrigation.
