# Chapter 20: Climate Psychology and Mental Health

*Theme: climate anxiety, trauma and building psychological resilience*

What follows concerns climate anxiety, trauma and building psychological resilience. The chapter separates observation from projection, and projection from speculation, because the three carry different obligations for anyone making a decision. Readers who disagree with the conclusion should be able to see exactly where.

## 20.1 Climate Anxiety and Rational Alarm

Kristie Ebi (2018) puts it plainly: if you have cancer, you need the doctor to tell you how serious it is — people are suffering and dying right now; risks rise without urgent investment in health systems.

Ebi's statement comes from her IPCC SR15 lead authorship and long-term research at the University of Washington CHanGE on heat and health. 'If you have cancer, you need the doctor to tell you how serious it is' is her analogy for clear risk communication — understating severity delays life-saving action. 'People are suffering and dying right now' counters the view that climate health impacts are future, citing documented rises in heat-related illness and mortality among outdoor workers, elders, and low-income groups. 'Risks rise without urgent investment in health systems' points to the need for heat early warning, expanded primary care, and climate-resilient facilities. Ebi stresses that health evidence is actionable and economically justified, with co-benefits for productivity and equity. For climate anxiety and rational alarm within climate anxiety, her framing treats adaptation as public health preparedness, requiring surveillance, workforce training, and funding that reaches the front lines. For climate anxiety and rational alarm, the practical consequence is to name the decision that would have to change.

Most disagreements about climate anxiety and rational alarm are not disagreements about facts. They are disagreements about weight: which risks count, over what horizon, and for whom (IPCC, 2022). That is why the same evidence can support both alarm and complacency without either side misreading the data. The scale involved is not marginal: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Within mental-health services and schools, clarity about the weighting matters as much as clarity about the measurement. Stating the criterion in advance is what separates analysis from advocacy.

The evidence base behind climate anxiety and rational alarm is larger than its public profile suggests, and more uneven. Satellite records, station data, insurance claims and household surveys answer different questions, and they rarely agree on the same decimal place (IPCC, 2021). Where they disagree, the disagreement is usually about coverage rather than about the direction of change. Confidence is highest on aggregates and lowest on localities: ~7 million air-pollution deaths occur annually (WHO, 2023). As far as mental-health services and schools is concerned, the sensible use of the record is to establish direction and then test local plausibility rather than to import a global average wholesale. A number that cannot be traced to a source is not evidence; it is decoration.

Why things behave as they do is a question with a traceable answer. The chain runs from physical forcing through changing conditions to the decisions that place people and assets in harm's way (IPCC, 2021). Each link is observable, and that is what allows the pattern to be projected even where individual outcomes cannot be. The magnitudes involved are not small: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). In the work of mental-health services and schools, there is more than one point of action, and the cheapest interventions are usually the earliest. Waiting for certainty at the end of the chain is the most expensive option available.

Uncertainty in climate anxiety and rational alarm is not a single quantity, and treating it as one is the most common analytic error in the field. Physical uncertainty, scenario uncertainty and model uncertainty behave differently: the first narrows with data, the second depends on choices, the third on structure (IPCC, 2021). Only the first is reduced by waiting; the other two are reduced by deciding. The range is wide enough to matter for planning: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). For mental-health services and schools, the correct response to the wide part of the range is a decision that performs acceptably across it, not a postponement until it narrows. Plans that require certainty before they begin are plans that begin too late.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Heat waves in South Asia in 2022 and 2023 caused thousands of deaths and crop losses (IPCC, 2022; WMO, 2023). Set beside the record, the episode indicates direction without fixing the rate. Within mental-health services and schools, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

It is worth separating what would change the conclusion from what would merely add detail. A revision of the observed trend, or evidence that adaptation is failing faster than expected, would matter; another confirmation of the same direction would not. That test keeps attention on the claims that carry weight. Within mental-health services and schools, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 20.2 Youth Anxiety and Government Inaction

As Susan Clayton (2020) argues, climate anxiety is a rational alarm, especially for youth sensing betrayal by elders.

Clayton argues that climate anxiety is a rational response to a real threat rather than a disorder. Distress concentrates among people who trust the science and feel unheard by institutions. Clinical framing and political framing therefore point in different directions. Applied to youth anxiety and government inaction, the argument sets a test: does the measure still work when capacity is stretched?

The evidence base for youth anxiety and government inaction is broad but uneven, and the unevenness is informative. Where many independent studies agree, confidence is high; where a single model family supplies the estimate, it is not (IPCC, 2021). One figure illustrates the state of knowledge: ~7 million air-pollution deaths occur annually (WHO, 2023). The appropriate response to a strong signal with weak local detail is not to wait for precision but to design for the range. As far as mental-health services and schools is concerned, that means carrying uncertainty into the decision rather than resolving it beforehand. Plans that require certainty before acting are plans that act late.

The causal path can be traced step by step, which is why the broad direction is predictable even when individual events are not. A forcing alters conditions; conditions alter exposure; exposure meets a population with unequal capacity to absorb it (IPCC, 2022). Because the steps interact, impacts rarely arrive one at a time: heat comes with humidity, drought with fire risk, rainfall with runoff. The same pattern is visible in the numbers: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). In the work of mental-health services and schools, the practical implication is to plan for combinations rather than single hazards. Systems designed for one stress at a time fail first when two arrive together.

An honest account of youth anxiety and government inaction distinguishes three things: what has been measured, what has been modelled, and what has been assumed. Most public confusion comes from collapsing the three into one sentence (IPCC, 2021). The distinction is concrete: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Numbers carry confidence intervals, and those intervals should travel with them into any decision. For mental-health services and schools, the discipline is to write the interval next to the estimate whenever the estimate is used. A disclosed range can be planned against; an undisclosed point estimate cannot.

Discussion of youth anxiety and government inaction usually begins as a technical question, and that framing hides the decisions underneath it. The technical part is real: measurement, modelling and attribution have improved sharply over two decades (IPCC, 2023). But the contested part is rarely the measurement; it is what the numbers imply for who acts, who pays and who is protected. One figure sets the scale: ~7 million air-pollution deaths occur annually (WHO, 2023). Read that way, the question for mental-health services and schools is not whether the trend is real but which choices remain defensible across the plausible range. A framing that treats uncertainty as a reason to wait gets the logic backwards: uncertainty is what makes early, reversible decisions valuable. Within mental-health services and schools, the test is whether the arrangement survives contact with a bad week.

Current reporting adds detail rather than a new direction: European summer 2022 heatwave caused over 61,000 excess deaths, the continent's deadliest heat event (Nature Medicine, 2023). Read against the longer record, it is an illustration rather than a proof. As far as mental-health services and schools is concerned, what matters is not the record but the lead time it implies.

Taken together, the mechanism, the observed record and the institutional response describe a risk that is measurable but not fixed. The practical question is not whether the trend is real, but which decisions today remain defensible across the full range of projections — and those are usually the decisions that are cheap to reverse. As far as mental-health services and schools is concerned, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 20.3 Trauma, PTSD and Mental Health Impacts

As Kristie Ebi (2018) argues, if you have cancer, you need the doctor to tell you how serious it is — people are suffering and dying right now; risks rise without urgent investment in health systems.

Ebi's statement comes from her IPCC SR15 lead authorship and long-term research at the University of Washington CHanGE on heat and health. 'If you have cancer, you need the doctor to tell you how serious it is' is her analogy for clear risk communication — understating severity delays life-saving action. 'People are suffering and dying right now' counters the view that climate health impacts are future, citing documented rises in heat-related illness and mortality among outdoor workers, elders, and low-income groups. 'Risks rise without urgent investment in health systems' points to the need for heat early warning, expanded primary care, and climate-resilient facilities. Ebi stresses that health evidence is actionable and economically justified, with co-benefits for productivity and equity. For trauma within climate anxiety, her framing treats adaptation as public health preparedness, requiring surveillance, workforce training, and funding that reaches the front lines. For trauma, the bearing is direct: the constraint is institutional as much as physical.

Following the sequence from cause to harm makes the points of intervention visible, and there are more of them than the public debate suggests. Emissions determine the forcing; land use and insurance determine exposure; income, health and warning determine capacity; each link can be altered by policy at a different scale (UNDRR, 2015). Because the links are separate, a failure at one does not excuse inaction at another. The scale at which the chain operates is large: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). In the work of mental-health services and schools, the practical value of thinking in a chain is that it shows which link is cheapest to move this year. Intervention points are political choices, not physical facts.

Not all statements about trauma, PTSD and mental health impacts carry the same warrant. Some rest on direct measurement, some on models, some on expert judgement, and the three should not be written in the same voice (IPCC, 2021). The numbers are often firmer than the uses to which they are put: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Misreading usually happens in translation — a global average applied to a locality, a scenario treated as a forecast. For mental-health services and schools, the safeguard is to record the source, period and assumptions behind each figure used. Traceability is not bureaucracy; it is what allows a claim to be checked later.

How a problem is framed decides what counts as a solution, and trauma, PTSD and mental health impacts is a case in point. Framed as an environmental issue, it invites a ministry and a budget line; framed as a development issue, it invites land use, housing and finance at once (IPCC, 2022). The second framing is harder to administer precisely because it cannot be assigned to a single department. The scale involved rules out a narrow frame: ~7 million air-pollution deaths occur annually (WHO, 2023). Within mental-health services and schools, the practical consequence is that the frame has to be chosen before the plan, not after it. Frames are cheap to change on paper and expensive to change in an institution.

The record on trauma, PTSD and mental health impacts is now strong enough to be treated as a trend rather than an anomaly. The record is uneven — some regions and seasons move sharply, others barely — but the aggregate direction is consistent across methods (IPCC, 2023). The headline figure is blunt: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). What remains contested is attribution of individual events and the speed of future change, not whether the baseline has shifted. That distinction shapes how mental-health services and schools should read the numbers: as a distribution to plan against, not a forecast to await. Where the evidence is thin, the honest response is a stated limitation rather than a confident sentence. As far as mental-health services and schools is concerned, the practical question is what would have to change first.

A recent marker points the same way: Heat waves in South Asia in 2022 and 2023 caused thousands of deaths and crop losses (IPCC, 2022; WMO, 2023). The value of such reports is that they date the change rather than explain it. In the work of mental-health services and schools, the item is useful mainly as a date stamp on a trend.

Certainty is unevenly distributed across this literature. Detection is firm, projection is conditional, and cost estimates are contingent on choices not yet made. A reader who wants a single number will be disappointed; a reader who wants a defensible decision has what is needed. In the work of mental-health services and schools, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 20.4 Building Psychological Resilience

Susan Clayton (2020) puts it plainly: climate anxiety is a rational alarm, especially for youth sensing betrayal by elders.

Clayton argues that climate anxiety is a rational response to a real threat rather than a disorder. Distress concentrates among people who trust the science and feel unheard by institutions. Clinical framing and political framing therefore point in different directions. Read against building psychological resilience, the point is that intention without capacity changes little.

The strength of the claim about building psychological resilience varies, and that variation should be visible in the prose. Detection and attribution are well supported; regional projections carry wider bands; estimates of future damage depend on assumptions about emissions and adaptation (IPCC, 2021). A concrete example of the difference: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). A central estimate without its range is not more precise, only less honest. For mental-health services and schools, the discipline is to keep scenarios labelled as scenarios and observations labelled as observations. Where the evidence is thin, the correct response is a stated limitation rather than a confident sentence.

It helps to ask what question building psychological resilience is really answering. Framed as a measurement problem, it invites better instruments; framed as a decision problem, it invites better institutions (IPCC, 2023). The difference is practical, because the second framing admits trade-offs that the first hides. The scale is already visible: ~7 million air-pollution deaths occur annually (WHO, 2023). Within mental-health services and schools, the choice of framing determines which options are considered at all. A question that excludes trade-offs will produce an answer that cannot be implemented.

Evidence on building psychological resilience rests on several independent lines rather than a single study. Satellites, ground stations, ocean measurements and model reanalyses now agree on direction, and disagree mainly on pace and regional detail (IPCC, 2021). One measure puts the magnitude in view: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). Confidence is highest where those lines converge and lowest where one method or a short record carries the claim. As far as mental-health services and schools is concerned, that means planning against a range rather than a point. A decision built on the central estimate alone is a decision that will be surprised.

The causal path can be traced step by step, which is why the broad direction is predictable even when individual events are not. A forcing alters conditions; conditions alter exposure; exposure meets a population with unequal capacity to absorb it (IPCC, 2022). Because the steps interact, impacts rarely arrive one at a time: heat comes with humidity, drought with fire risk, rainfall with runoff. The same pattern is visible in the numbers: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). In the work of mental-health services and schools, the practical implication is to plan for combinations rather than single hazards. Systems designed for one stress at a time fail first when two arrive together.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: European summer 2022 heatwave caused over 61,000 excess deaths, the continent's deadliest heat event (Nature Medicine, 2023). It matters less as a record than as evidence that the baseline has already moved. For mental-health services and schools, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

What emerges is less a prediction than a test. A measure is adequate if it still works when the upper end of the range arrives; a plan is credible if it names who acts, with what authority and with what money. Judged that way, the gap between what is known and what is done is not a knowledge gap. For mental-health services and schools, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 20.5 Case Study: Climate Psychology and Mental Health

A single episode illustrates the mechanism: Heat waves in South Asia in 2022 and 2023 caused thousands of deaths and crop losses (IPCC, 2022; WMO, 2023). Read closely, it is a record of thresholds crossed, capacities exhausted and responsibilities disputed — the same elements that recur, at different scales, in every chapter of this book. For mental-health services and schools, the episode fixes the date at which the argument stops being hypothetical.

Cases of this kind are useful because they compress several mechanisms into a short period. Exposure, capacity and institutional reach become visible at once, and the gaps between them stop being abstract (UNDRR, 2015). Within mental-health services and schools, the episode works as a readiness test rather than a weather story.

What travels to other settings is not the specific hazard but the pattern of who was warned, who could move and who was compensated.



## 20.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Climate Psychology and Mental Health

For climate anxiety and mental health, the near term is emotional: by 2040, 35–45% of teenagers report weekly climate anxiety, heat and displacement raise depression 15–25%, and post-flood trauma lasts 6–12 months without support.

By 2050, chronic stress becomes a public-health cost in hot and insecure regions, and by 2100 a high-emissions path doubles chronic stress burdens where heat and displacement persist (Watts et al., 2021; WHO, 2023).

Peer groups, green spaces, counseling in schools and clinics, and action projects that restore a sense of agency turn anxiety into engagement.

[[TABLE:Table 20.1 Future scenarios and predictions for climate anxiety, trauma and building psychological resilience (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | anxiety 35–45% of teens weekly |
| 2050 | chronic stress raises health costs |
| 2100 | chronic burden ×2 in hot, insecure zones |

Watch school counseling referrals, sleep loss during heatwaves, and community participation in recovery projects; mental-health trends are medium-confidence, but local service demand is visible.

# Chapter 21: Governance, Justice and Civic Engagement

*Theme: climate governance, justice, equity and civic engagement*

This chapter examines climate governance, justice, equity and civic engagement. It moves from mechanism to evidence to what can be done, and each section states its main claim before the supporting material. Where the evidence is uncertain, the text says so; where it is strong, the language is correspondingly direct.

## 21.1 International Frameworks and Paris Agreement

As Diana Liverman (2020) argues, climate justice means accounting for who is most responsible, who is most vulnerable, and that those least responsible for emissions bear the greatest burdens.

Liverman's climate justice scholarship, including her 2019 testimony to the Arizona House and IPCC authorship, underpins her statement on responsibility and vulnerability. 'Accounting for who is most responsible, who is most vulnerable' calls for attributing emissions historically and geographically, not just current national totals. 'Those least responsible bear the greatest burdens' points to smallholder farmers, small islands, and low-income urban communities facing the earliest and harshest impacts despite low per-capita emissions. Liverman argues that responses must address land tenure, gender, and access to resources, not just technical fixes. She emphasizes that top-down prescriptions often fail when they ignore local knowledge and power imbalances. For international frameworks and paris agreement within climate governance, her lens requires assessing interventions by who benefits, who pays, and who decides, integrating equity metrics into planning and evaluation. In the terms of international frameworks and paris agreement, the claim shifts attention from prediction to preparation.

The case for spending early rests on a comparison that is rarely made explicit: prevention against recovery, over decades rather than one budget cycle. Damage functions and observed recovery costs point the same way (World Bank, 2022). The order of magnitude is contested in detail but not in direction: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Prevention loses politically because its benefits are invisible: the disaster that did not happen has no constituency. For courts, legislatures and civic organisations, the fix is institutional — budget rules that reserve funds for prevention, and appraisal that counts avoided losses. Otherwise the arithmetic is sound and the incentives are not.

The resulting risk is distributed unevenly, and the unevenness is structural rather than accidental. Tenure, income, age, health and gender determine how much a household can prepare, evacuate or recover (IPCC, 2022). The aggregate numbers conceal this: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). Averages describe the size of the problem, not who carries it. Decisions taken by courts, legislatures and civic organisations — where to build, whom to insure, which services keep running — widen that gap or close it. A policy that protects the average household while leaving the most exposed unprotected has not solved the problem it claims to solve. Within courts, legislatures and civic organisations, the implication is a shorter list of decisions than the analysis suggests.

Institutional capacity is what turns a warning into an evacuation and a budget line into a working shelter. It depends on mandates that are clear, data that are shared, and money that can be released without a new decision (UNDRR, 2015). Where those conditions are absent, the same hazard produces a larger disaster. The stakes are visible in the numbers: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). As far as courts, legislatures and civic organisations is concerned, capability is better tested in a drill than in a document. A plan that has never been exercised is a hypothesis.

The next decade will show whether preparation or improvisation prevails. Three signals are worth tracking: how often events exceed design thresholds, how much public spending goes to prevention rather than repair, and whether the most exposed groups gain access before a disaster rather than after it (IPCC, 2023). The baseline is already shifting: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). None of those signals is a perfect measure, but together they indicate direction. In the work of courts, legislatures and civic organisations, adaptation is not a state to be reached but a standing capacity to revise decisions as evidence changes. The test is whether the next surprise is met with a plan or with a scramble.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Hurricane Ian (Sept 2022) caused $113 billion in US damages and was the costliest Florida hurricane (NOAA NHC). Reported in isolation the figure looks dramatic; reported against the baseline it looks consistent. As far as courts, legislatures and civic organisations is concerned, what matters is not the record but the lead time it implies.

Two limits apply. The first is scale: global figures say little about a district, and district figures are often unavailable. The second is time: the record is long enough to detect a trend but not long enough to rule out rare extremes. Good planning treats both as design constraints rather than reasons to postpone. For courts, legislatures and civic organisations, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 21.2 National Adaptation Plans and Local Governance

As Mark Howden (2020) argues, adapting Agriculture to Climate Change)

Howden's statement comes from his 2007 PNAS paper 'Adapting agriculture to climate change' and his role as IPCC WGII Vice-Chair. 'Dealing with barriers to effective adaptation will require a comprehensive and dynamic policy approach' means technical options alone fail when institutions, markets, and knowledge systems are misaligned. 'Science, too, has to adapt' calls for linking scientific, local, and Indigenous knowledge, and for research that is co-produced with farmers and communities. Barriers he identifies include fragmented governance, short-term incentives, and poor access to tailored forecasts and finance. Howden emphasizes iterative learning — piloting, monitoring, and adjusting — rather than one-off plans. For national adaptation plans and local governance within climate governance, this directs attention to enabling conditions for adoption: trusted extension, tenure security, and policy that supports diversification and ecosystem-based practices alongside technology. For national adaptation plans and local governance, the practical consequence is to name the decision that would have to change.

The aggregate picture hides the distribution, and in national adaptation plans and local governance the distribution carries most of the policy content. Risk is concentrated by geography, tenure and income, and the concentration is produced by prior decisions about land, services and insurance (UNDRR, 2015). Because those decisions were made deliberately, they can be unmade deliberately. The unevenness is measurable: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). Within courts, legislatures and civic organisations, averages are the wrong unit of account; the relevant figure is the burden carried by the group least able to carry it. A policy judged by its average effect will systematically miss the people it is meant to protect.

Governance is tested at the seams between organisations. Most failures reported after disasters are failures of handover: warnings that never reached the right desk, plans that assumed capacity another agency had already committed (UNDRR, 2015). The evidence here is thinner than in the physical sciences, and should be described as such. One indication of the stakes: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). What the record does show is that authority, money and information have to move together; any one without the others stalls. As far as courts, legislatures and civic organisations is concerned, the test is whether a decision can be taken where the consequence is felt, with the resources to act on it.

Anyone deciding about national adaptation plans and local governance is choosing under conditions that will not improve before the choice has to be made. That makes sequencing the central skill: measures that are cheap, reversible and useful under several futures go first, and irreversible commitments wait for better information (IPCC, 2022). The sequence matters because early options buy the time that later options need. The pressure is documented rather than projected: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). In the work of courts, legislatures and civic organisations, the test of a plan is not whether it is optimal for one forecast but whether it survives several. Decisions taken now will be judged by events that were not in the forecast.

Money spent before an event buys more protection than money spent after it. That claim rests on damage functions and on the repeated observation that recovery costs exceed prevention costs by a wide margin (World Bank, 2022). The order of magnitude is not in doubt: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). What is uncertain is how benefits are distributed and how quickly they appear, which is why appraisal methods matter. For courts, legislatures and civic organisations, the relevant question is not whether prevention pays but who captures the return. Financing arrangements that ignore that question tend to fund visible damage and neglect invisible capacity.

Current reporting adds detail rather than a new direction: Flooding in Pakistan in 2022 affected 33 million people, killed >1,700 and caused over $30 billion in damages/losses (World Bank PDNA 28 Oct 2022). On its own it proves little; placed against the trend it shows the direction of travel. In the work of courts, legislatures and civic organisations, the item is useful mainly as a date stamp on a trend.

None of this settles the political question of how much to spend and who should pay. It does settle a prior question: whether there is anything worth spending on. The evidence says there is, and that the returns depend on acting while the options are still open. Within courts, legislatures and civic organisations, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 21.3 Carbon Pricing and Climate Finance

Francesco La Camera (2023) puts it plainly: renewables dominate new power, but deployment must triple by 2030 — finance is the choke point.

IRENA documents that renewables dominate new generating capacity but deployment must roughly triple by 2030 to meet agreed targets. Finance, grids and permits are the binding constraints. Cost is no longer the main obstacle in most markets. Applied to carbon pricing and climate finance, the argument sets a test: does the measure still work when capacity is stretched?

Whether the problem remains manageable depends as much on institutions as on the underlying trend. Early warning, land-use rules, standards and social protection reduce harm, but only where authority and funding reach the level at which risk is felt (UNDRR, 2015). The common failure is fragmentation: overlapping mandates, unshared data, responsibility falling between agencies. Coordination is a design problem with measurable features — joint protocols, common baselines, clear triggers. As far as courts, legislatures and civic organisations is concerned, the test is whether its own arrangements have those features before the next event rather than after it. Where such arrangements exist, response times fall and losses follow.

The relevant question for carbon pricing and climate finance is not what will happen but what to do while the answer is still forming. That favours measures that are cheap, reversible and useful across several futures. Early warning, insurance, redundant systems and protected water sources meet that test; megaprojects tied to one scenario do not (IPCC, 2023). Pressure is building regardless: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). In the work of courts, legislatures and civic organisations, the portfolio matters more than any single measure. Robustness, not optimality, is the right criterion under uncertainty.

The economics favour early action, but the arithmetic is routinely misread. Damage functions show rising marginal losses with warming, while preventive spending delivers returns over decades (World Bank, 2022). The magnitudes are large: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). The obstacle is less the total than the distribution of costs and benefits: those who pay are rarely those who are protected. For courts, legislatures and civic organisations, the implication is to count avoided losses, not only capital outlays. Insurance can spread the residual risk; it cannot reduce the hazard itself.

The distribution of harm is not a detail appended to the analysis; it is the analysis. Two households can face the same event and emerge with different futures, because what they own, owe and can claim determines recovery (IPCC, 2022). The aggregate picture obscures this: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). For that reason, average-based targets can be met while the most exposed end up worse off. Within courts, legislatures and civic organisations, the measures that matter are those judged by their effect on the bottom of the distribution, not the middle. That is also the test of whether a policy is serious.

A recent marker points the same way: Hurricane Ian (Sept 2022) caused $113 billion in US damages and was the costliest Florida hurricane (NOAA NHC). Taken alone the episode is weather; taken with the record it is a data point on a slope. For courts, legislatures and civic organisations, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

The evidence is not unanimous on pace and regional detail, and the gaps should be named rather than smoothed over. Where estimates differ, the difference usually traces back to assumptions about emissions, adaptation and measurement. That does not weaken the case for action; it defines the range within which action has to be robust. As far as courts, legislatures and civic organisations is concerned, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 21.4 Adaptation Finance Gaps and Investment

As Inger Andersen (2023) argues, adaptation finance gaps are widening, leaving the vulnerable to pay with lives.

UNEP's adaptation-gap reports compare finance needs with flows actually disbursed. The gap has widened every year the series has been published. Adaptation remains the smaller share of climate finance by a wide margin. For adaptation finance gaps and investment, the bearing is direct: the constraint is institutional as much as physical.

Any assessment of adaptation finance gaps and investment will turn less on what is promised now than on what is in place when the next event arrives. Plans that exist only on paper, thresholds that trigger no action, and funds that cannot be released quickly are the recurring failure modes (UNDRR, 2015). The pressure is not hypothetical: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Progress can be measured in lead times, in the share of exposed households covered by warning and insurance, and in how quickly services return. In the work of courts, legislatures and civic organisations, reviewing those measures on a fixed cycle works better than reviewing them after each disaster. Iteration is the difference between adaptation and repeated recovery.

The economics of adaptation finance gaps and investment are usually presented as a bill, which is the least useful form in which to present them. Costs arrive early and are budgeted, while benefits arrive late and are unassigned; the asymmetry systematically biases public investment toward response and away from preparation (World Bank, 2023). Insurance and credit then transmit that bias into private decisions about where to build and what to protect. The magnitude is not marginal: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). For courts, legislatures and civic organisations, the useful accounting separates the cost of preparing from the cost of not preparing, and prices both over the same horizon. A measure that is expensive against one budget year is often cheap against ten.

The burden falls first on people with the least room to manoeuvre. Those with insecure tenure, irregular income or caring responsibilities have less capacity to move, insure or wait for compensation (IPCC, 2022). The scale is not marginal: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). Because disadvantages compound, the same event can be an inconvenience for one family and a permanent loss for another. Within courts, legislatures and civic organisations, equity is therefore a design criterion rather than a footnote. Measures that reach the most exposed first tend to perform better for everyone else as well.

Institutional performance in adaptation finance gaps and investment depends less on formal authority than on whether mandates, money and information arrive together. Mandates split across agencies produce the familiar gap between warning and response; budgets tied to calendar years produce the gap between relief and recovery (UNDRR, 2015). Neither gap requires bad faith, only an ordinary division of labour. The strain is already visible in the numbers: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). As far as courts, legislatures and civic organisations is concerned, the design question is where to place the authority that can move money and people within the same week. Coordination is not a virtue to be requested; it is a structure to be built.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Flooding in Pakistan in 2022 affected 33 million people, killed >1,700 and caused over $30 billion in damages/losses (World Bank PDNA 28 Oct 2022). The item is useful because it fixes a date to a trend that otherwise drifts. Within courts, legislatures and civic organisations, what matters is not the record but the lead time it implies.

The strands point in one direction: the physical signal is established, the human consequences depend on exposure and capacity, and the response depends on decisions taken before the event. That sequence is what makes the problem tractable, and what makes delay expensive. In the work of courts, legislatures and civic organisations, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 21.5 Case Study: Governance, Justice and Civic Engagement

The case that anchors this chapter is documented and recent: Hurricane Ian (Sept 2022) caused $113 billion in US damages and was the costliest Florida hurricane (NOAA NHC). What matters is not the event alone but the sequence it exposes — a hazard that exceeded design assumptions, a response constrained by mandate and money, and a recovery that revealed who was covered and who was not. Within courts, legislatures and civic organisations, it is the clearest recent test of whether the arrangements on paper survive contact with an event.

The instructive part is the asymmetry between the event and its aftermath. Response times are measured in hours, recovery in years, and the difference between the two is usually decided before the event — by land use, insurance and local authority, all of which courts, legislatures and civic organisations controls.

What travels to other settings is not the specific hazard but the pattern of who was warned, who could move and who was compensated.



## 21.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Governance, Justice and Civic Engagement

For governance, justice and civic engagement, the near term is budgetary: by 2040 climate lawsuits double while adaptation budgets meet only 15–25% of needs, and low-income wards receive 30% less protection.

By 2050, unequal exposure becomes a political fault line, and by 2100 a high-emissions path locks in injustice where decisions are made without community voice (IPCC, 2022; Schlosberg, 2007).

Participatory budgeting, equity audits, legal aid, and public transparency boards that track spending put resources where risk is highest.

[[TABLE:Table 21.1 Future scenarios and predictions for climate governance, justice, equity and civic engagement (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | adaptation budgets 15–25% of needs |
| 2050 | unequal exposure becomes political |
| 2100 | injustice locked in without voice |

Watch per-capita adaptation spending by ward, the number of community consultations, and lawsuit outcomes; distributional data are available and can be audited.

# Chapter 22: Case Studies: Heat, Fire, Flood and Urban Extremes

*Theme: regional case studies of heat, wildfire, flood and urban climate extremes*

The subject of this chapter is regional case studies of heat, wildfire, flood and urban climate extremes. The argument proceeds in three steps: what is happening, how we know, and what follows for decisions. Sources are cited as they are used, and contested points are marked as contested rather than presented as settled.

## 22.1 Heat in South Asia and Urban Heat in Global Cities

As Friederike Otto (2021) argues, the Pacific Northwest 2021 heatwave would have been virtually impossible without human-caused climate change.

Otto's statement comes from the 2021 World Weather Attribution rapid study of the Pacific Northwest heat dome, which found such heat virtually impossible without climate change. 'Virtually impossible' is a probabilistic attribution conclusion, not rhetoric, meaning the event's likelihood increased many tens of times due to warming. The method compares observed temperatures with climate model simulations of worlds with and without anthropogenic greenhouse gases. The heatwave — with Lytton, Canada reaching 49.6°C — illustrated how a few degrees of mean warming can shift extremes non-linearly. Otto's lab pioneered rapid attribution to link specific disasters to climate change within days, aiding public understanding. The finding underscores that heat in south asia and urban heat in global cities extremes are not random bad luck but part of a detectable trend. For regional case studies of heat, it strengthens the case for updating standards for infrastructure, health, and labor based on new extremes, not historical averages. The implication for heat in south asia and urban heat in global cities is a question of sequence: what has to be in place before the event, not after it.

Uncertainty in heat in south asia and urban heat in global cities is not a single quantity, and treating it as one is the most common analytic error in the field. Physical uncertainty, scenario uncertainty and model uncertainty behave differently: the first narrows with data, the second depends on choices, the third on structure (IPCC, 2021). Only the first is reduced by waiting; the other two are reduced by deciding. The range is wide enough to matter for planning: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). In the work of urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions, the correct response to the wide part of the range is a decision that performs acceptably across it, not a postponement until it narrows. Plans that require certainty before they begin are plans that begin too late.

Most disagreements about heat in south asia and urban heat in global cities are not disagreements about facts. They are disagreements about weight: which risks count, over what horizon, and for whom (IPCC, 2022). That is why the same evidence can support both alarm and complacency without either side misreading the data. The scale involved is not marginal: sea level has risen ~20–25 cm since 1900 (IPCC, 2021). For urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions, clarity about the weighting matters as much as clarity about the measurement. Stating the criterion in advance is what separates analysis from advocacy.

The evidence base behind heat in south asia and urban heat in global cities is larger than its public profile suggests, and more uneven. Satellite records, station data, insurance claims and household surveys answer different questions, and they rarely agree on the same decimal place (IPCC, 2021). Where they disagree, the disagreement is usually about coverage rather than about the direction of change. Confidence is highest on aggregates and lowest on localities: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). Within urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions, the sensible use of the record is to establish direction and then test local plausibility rather than to import a global average wholesale. A number that cannot be traced to a source is not evidence; it is decoration.

Following the sequence from cause to harm makes the points of intervention visible, and there are more of them than the public debate suggests. Emissions determine the forcing; land use and insurance determine exposure; income, health and warning determine capacity; each link can be altered by policy at a different scale (UNDRR, 2015). Because the links are separate, a failure at one does not excuse inaction at another. The scale at which the chain operates is large: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). As far as urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions is concerned, the practical value of thinking in a chain is that it shows which link is cheapest to move this year. Intervention points are political choices, not physical facts.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Wildfires in Canada in 2023 burned ~15 million ha, more than double the previous record (1989, 6.7 Mha), sending smoke across North America (CIFFC, Nature 2024). Read against the longer record, it is an illustration rather than a proof. In the work of urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions, the item is useful mainly as a date stamp on a trend.

Readers should know where this section stands on the evidence ladder. The direction of change is established; the pace is estimated; the local consequences are modelled. Statements that blur those levels are the ones most likely to mislead, and they are also the easiest to check. In the work of urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 22.2 Wildfires in Australia and North America

Friederike Otto (2021) puts it plainly: the Pacific Northwest 2021 heatwave would have been virtually impossible without human-caused climate change.

Otto's statement comes from the 2021 World Weather Attribution rapid study of the Pacific Northwest heat dome, which found such heat virtually impossible without climate change. 'Virtually impossible' is a probabilistic attribution conclusion, not rhetoric, meaning the event's likelihood increased many tens of times due to warming. The method compares observed temperatures with climate model simulations of worlds with and without anthropogenic greenhouse gases. The heatwave — with Lytton, Canada reaching 49.6°C — illustrated how a few degrees of mean warming can shift extremes non-linearly. Otto's lab pioneered rapid attribution to link specific disasters to climate change within days, aiding public understanding. The finding underscores that wildfires in australia and north america extremes are not random bad luck but part of a detectable trend. For regional case studies of heat, it strengthens the case for updating standards for infrastructure, health, and labor based on new extremes, not historical averages. In the terms of wildfires in australia and north america, the claim shifts attention from prediction to preparation.

Discussion of wildfires in australia and north america usually begins as a technical question, and that framing hides the decisions underneath it. The technical part is real: measurement, modelling and attribution have improved sharply over two decades (IPCC, 2023). But the contested part is rarely the measurement; it is what the numbers imply for who acts, who pays and who is protected. One figure sets the scale: sea level has risen ~20–25 cm since 1900 (IPCC, 2021). Read that way, the question for urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions is not whether the trend is real but which choices remain defensible across the plausible range. A framing that treats uncertainty as a reason to wait gets the logic backwards: uncertainty is what makes early, reversible decisions valuable. For urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions, that is the difference between a plan that can be defended and one that merely exists.

The evidence base for wildfires in australia and north america is broad but uneven, and the unevenness is informative. Where many independent studies agree, confidence is high; where a single model family supplies the estimate, it is not (IPCC, 2021). One figure illustrates the state of knowledge: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). The appropriate response to a strong signal with weak local detail is not to wait for precision but to design for the range. Within urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions, that means carrying uncertainty into the decision rather than resolving it beforehand. Plans that require certainty before acting are plans that act late.

Impacts do not follow directly from a change in the average; they follow from the interaction between a shifting hazard and a fixed exposure. A house built to a hundred-year flood standard meets a twenty-five-year event with alarming regularity once the distribution has moved (IPCC, 2022). Nothing about the house changed; the standard it was designed against did. The shift is quantifiable: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). As far as urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions is concerned, the lesson is that design standards have a shelf life and need a review date attached to them. An unrevised standard is a decision to accept a higher level of risk than the one originally debated.

An honest account of wildfires in australia and north america distinguishes three things: what has been measured, what has been modelled, and what has been assumed. Most public confusion comes from collapsing the three into one sentence (IPCC, 2021). The distinction is concrete: sea level has risen ~20–25 cm since 1900 (IPCC, 2021). Numbers carry confidence intervals, and those intervals should travel with them into any decision. In the work of urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions, the discipline is to write the interval next to the estimate whenever the estimate is used. A disclosed range can be planned against; an undisclosed point estimate cannot.

Current reporting adds detail rather than a new direction: Spain's 2023 olive harvest halved after drought+heat, driving global olive oil prices up 112% (FAO, IOC). The value of such reports is that they date the change rather than explain it. For urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

What the section establishes is narrower than a prediction and firmer than an opinion. It establishes that the direction is known, that the costs of inaction are concentrated on those least able to bear them, and that the available measures are known well enough to be tested. For urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 22.3 Floods in Southeast Asia and Urban Flooding

As Chuang Zhao (2017) argues, each degree of warming lowers staple yields by ~6%, with wheat most vulnerable to heat.

Zhao and colleagues combined field warming experiments with models to estimate yield responses per degree. Staples fall by roughly six per cent per degree of local warming, with wheat most sensitive. The estimate applies to present-day locations and practices. For floods in southeast asia and urban flooding, the practical consequence is to name the decision that would have to change.

The record on floods in southeast asia and urban flooding is now strong enough to be treated as a trend rather than an anomaly. The record is uneven — some regions and seasons move sharply, others barely — but the aggregate direction is consistent across methods (IPCC, 2023). The headline figure is blunt: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). What remains contested is attribution of individual events and the speed of future change, not whether the baseline has shifted. That distinction shapes how urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions should read the numbers: as a distribution to plan against, not a forecast to await. Where the evidence is thin, the honest response is a stated limitation rather than a confident sentence. Within urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions, the test is whether the arrangement survives contact with a bad week.

Between the physical change and the harm there is a sequence, and each step can be examined on its own. A shift in the mean moves the tail of the distribution first; the tail meets exposure; exposure meets capacity; capacity fails at thresholds rather than gradually (IPCC, 2022). That is why impacts arrive in jumps and why the same event produces different outcomes in neighbouring districts. The arithmetic of the shift is blunt: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). As far as urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions is concerned, the useful question is which threshold comes first and what happens on the day it is crossed. Thresholds are where a gradual problem becomes a sudden one.

Not all statements about floods in southeast asia and urban flooding carry the same warrant. Some rest on direct measurement, some on models, some on expert judgement, and the three should not be written in the same voice (IPCC, 2021). The numbers are often firmer than the uses to which they are put: sea level has risen ~20–25 cm since 1900 (IPCC, 2021). Misreading usually happens in translation — a global average applied to a locality, a scenario treated as a forecast. In the work of urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions, the safeguard is to record the source, period and assumptions behind each figure used. Traceability is not bureaucracy; it is what allows a claim to be checked later.

How a problem is framed decides what counts as a solution, and floods in southeast asia and urban flooding is a case in point. Framed as an environmental issue, it invites a ministry and a budget line; framed as a development issue, it invites land use, housing and finance at once (IPCC, 2022). The second framing is harder to administer precisely because it cannot be assigned to a single department. The scale involved rules out a narrow frame: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). For urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions, the practical consequence is that the frame has to be chosen before the plan, not after it. Frames are cheap to change on paper and expensive to change in an institution.

A recent marker points the same way: Bangladesh's Sidr-like Cyclone Sitrang (Oct 2022) inundated 10 million coastal residents (Bangladesh BMD, UN OCHA). It matters less as a record than as evidence that the baseline has already moved. Within urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions, what matters is not the record but the lead time it implies.

It is worth separating what would change the conclusion from what would merely add detail. A revision of the observed trend, or evidence that adaptation is failing faster than expected, would matter; another confirmation of the same direction would not. That test keeps attention on the claims that carry weight. Within urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 22.4 Storms and Hurricanes in the Caribbean

As Ana Vicedo-Cabrera (2021) argues, over one-third of heat deaths are attributable to anthropogenic warming — a burden we can already count.

Vicedo-Cabrera and colleagues attributed a large share of warm-season heat deaths to anthropogenic warming using multi-country mortality records. The finding converts a global average into a body count. Attribution of this kind depends on the choice of baseline period. Applied to storms and hurricanes in the caribbean, the argument sets a test: does the measure still work when capacity is stretched?

The chain of causation is short enough to follow and long enough to intervene in. Physical change alters the frequency and intensity of events; exposure determines who meets them; capacity determines what happens next (IPCC, 2022). Because the links are separable, they can be addressed separately — and the cheapest link is usually exposure. The pattern is not hypothetical: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). As far as urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions is concerned, the lesson is to intervene where the leverage is greatest rather than where the damage is most visible. Response is necessary; prevention is cheaper.

The strength of the claim about storms and hurricanes in the caribbean varies, and that variation should be visible in the prose. Detection and attribution are well supported; regional projections carry wider bands; estimates of future damage depend on assumptions about emissions and adaptation (IPCC, 2021). A concrete example of the difference: sea level has risen ~20–25 cm since 1900 (IPCC, 2021). A central estimate without its range is not more precise, only less honest. In the work of urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions, the discipline is to keep scenarios labelled as scenarios and observations labelled as observations. Where the evidence is thin, the correct response is a stated limitation rather than a confident sentence.

It helps to ask what question storms and hurricanes in the caribbean is really answering. Framed as a measurement problem, it invites better instruments; framed as a decision problem, it invites better institutions (IPCC, 2023). The difference is practical, because the second framing admits trade-offs that the first hides. The scale is already visible: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). For urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions, the choice of framing determines which options are considered at all. A question that excludes trade-offs will produce an answer that cannot be implemented.

Evidence on storms and hurricanes in the caribbean rests on several independent lines rather than a single study. Satellites, ground stations, ocean measurements and model reanalyses now agree on direction, and disagree mainly on pace and regional detail (IPCC, 2021). One measure puts the magnitude in view: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). Confidence is highest where those lines converge and lowest where one method or a short record carries the claim. Within urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions, that means planning against a range rather than a point. A decision built on the central estimate alone is a decision that will be surprised.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Italy’s Emilia-Romagna floods (May 2023) caused 15 deaths and €8.5B damage after months of drought (Italy Civil Protection). Treated as a single observation it invites over-reading; treated as one of many it is informative. As far as urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions is concerned, the item is useful mainly as a date stamp on a trend.

Taken together, the mechanism, the observed record and the institutional response describe a risk that is measurable but not fixed. The practical question is not whether the trend is real, but which decisions today remain defensible across the full range of projections — and those are usually the decisions that are cheap to reverse. As far as urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions is concerned, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 22.5 Case Studies: Heat, Fire, Flood and Urban Extremes

One documented episode makes the argument concrete: Wildfires in Canada in 2023 burned ~15 million ha, more than double the previous record (1989, 6.7 Mha), sending smoke across North America (CIFFC, Nature 2024). It repays attention less as an outlier than as a chain of decisions — where the hazard was allowed to concentrate, how the response was organised, and who bore the residual loss. As far as urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions is concerned, the record supplies both the warning and the timetable.

Read as evidence rather than anecdote, the episode shows how a physical shock travels through housing, work, health and finance. Damage does not stop where the water recedes; it continues through lost income, interrupted schooling and depleted savings — a sequence urban authorities in fire- and flood-prone regions can measure in its own records.

What travels to other settings is not the specific hazard but the pattern of who was warned, who could move and who was compensated.



## 22.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Case Studies — Heat, Fire, Flood and Urban Extremes

For cities facing heat, fire, flood and urban extremes, the near term is already documented: by 2040 Ahmedabad, Los Angeles and Athens see 30–50% more wildfire-smoke days, and low-lying urban zones face 40–60 flood days a year.

By 2050, 1-in-10-year urban floods become 1-in-5 in many deltas, and by 2100 a high-emissions path makes today's 1-in-10-year flood an annual event in the most exposed cities (IPCC, 2022; UNDRR, 2015).

Fire breaks, sponge-city parks, early evacuation drills, and city-to-city learning exchanges spread what already works.

[[TABLE:Table 22.1 Future scenarios and predictions for regional case studies of heat, wildfire, flood and urban climate extremes (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | smoke days +30–40%; flood days +20 |
| 2050 | 1-in-10 urban flood → 1-in-5 |
| 2100 | 1-in-10 flood becomes annual |

Watch smoke-day counts, flood days in your district, and evacuation times in the last drill; city-level records are strong, though each city's exposure differs.
