# Chapter 23: Case Studies: Drought, Food, Water and Security

*Theme: regional case studies of drought, food crisis, water stress and security*

This chapter deals with regional case studies of drought, food crisis, water stress and security. Each section opens with a claim, tests it against the evidence and draws out the implication for the people who have to act on it. The aim is not completeness but a defensible line through a large literature.

## 23.1 Drought in the Horn of Africa and Sahel

Tim Wheeler (2013) puts it plainly: food shocks travel through prices and nutrition, striking import-dependent poor first.

Wheeler and von Braun trace how food shocks travel through prices, trade and nutrition. Import-dependent poor households absorb the first and deepest losses. Short-term food aid does not prevent the next shock. Read against drought in the horn of africa and sahel, the point is that intention without capacity changes little.

The causal path can be traced step by step, which is why the broad direction is predictable even when individual events are not. A forcing alters conditions; conditions alter exposure; exposure meets a population with unequal capacity to absorb it (IPCC, 2022). Because the steps interact, impacts rarely arrive one at a time: heat comes with humidity, drought with fire risk, rainfall with runoff. The same pattern is visible in the numbers: ~2 billion people lack safe water (WHO & UNICEF, 2023). As far as rural authorities in drought-prone regions is concerned, the practical implication is to plan for combinations rather than single hazards. Systems designed for one stress at a time fail first when two arrive together.

The distribution of harm is not a detail appended to the analysis; it is the analysis. Two households can face the same event and emerge with different futures, because what they own, owe and can claim determines recovery (IPCC, 2022). The aggregate picture obscures this: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). For that reason, average-based targets can be met while the most exposed end up worse off. In the work of rural authorities in drought-prone regions, the measures that matter are those judged by their effect on the bottom of the distribution, not the middle. That is also the test of whether a policy is serious.

Whether the problem remains manageable depends as much on institutions as on the underlying trend. Early warning, land-use rules, standards and social protection reduce harm, but only where authority and funding reach the level at which risk is felt (UNDRR, 2015). The common failure is fragmentation: overlapping mandates, unshared data, responsibility falling between agencies. Coordination is a design problem with measurable features — joint protocols, common baselines, clear triggers. For rural authorities in drought-prone regions, the test is whether its own arrangements have those features before the next event rather than after it. Where such arrangements exist, response times fall and losses follow.

The case for spending early rests on a comparison that is rarely made explicit: prevention against recovery, over decades rather than one budget cycle. Damage functions and observed recovery costs point the same way (World Bank, 2022). The order of magnitude is contested in detail but not in direction: ~2 billion people lack safe water (WHO & UNICEF, 2023). Prevention loses politically because its benefits are invisible: the disaster that did not happen has no constituency. Within rural authorities in drought-prone regions, the fix is institutional — budget rules that reserve funds for prevention, and appraisal that counts avoided losses. Otherwise the arithmetic is sound and the incentives are not.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Tonga's Hunga Tonga eruption (Jan 2022) injected unprecedented water vapor into stratosphere, affecting climate short-term (NASA). On its own it proves little; placed against the trend it shows the direction of travel. For rural authorities in drought-prone regions, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

Two cautions apply to everything in this section. First, most figures are aggregates, and aggregates travel badly between countries and neighbourhoods. Second, published estimates lag the climate they describe. Both cautions argue for revisiting the numbers rather than treating them as fixed. As far as rural authorities in drought-prone regions is concerned, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 23.2 Food Crisis in Sub-Saharan Africa and Price Volatility

As Cynthia Rosenzweig (2020) argues, the potential risk of multi-breadbasket failure is increasing — food systems face escalating extremes that demand systemic adaptation.

Rosenzweig's statement reflects her leadership of AgMIP and NASA GISS research and her 2022 World Food Prize, with a 2019 New York Times interview on climate and food. 'Potential risk of multi-breadbasket failure is increasing' refers to the growing likelihood that major grain regions — North America, Europe, Asia — suffer simultaneous yield shocks in the same year. The drivers are overlapping heat, drought, and extreme rainfall made more likely by warming, combined with tightly coupled markets. 'Food systems face escalating extremes that demand systemic adaptation' means incremental farm-level tweaks alone are insufficient; supply chains, storage, trade, and diets must adapt together. Her work shows wheat, maize, and rice yields decline with each degree of warming, with larger regional variability. For food crisis in sub-saharan africa and price volatility within regional case studies of drought, this frames food and agriculture as systemic risk requiring diversification, resilient varieties, ecosystem-based practices, and early action on storage and distribution. The implication for food crisis in sub-saharan africa and price volatility is a question of sequence: what has to be in place before the event, not after it.

The burden falls first on people with the least room to manoeuvre. Those with insecure tenure, irregular income or caring responsibilities have less capacity to move, insure or wait for compensation (IPCC, 2022). The scale is not marginal: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). Because disadvantages compound, the same event can be an inconvenience for one family and a permanent loss for another. In the work of rural authorities in drought-prone regions, equity is therefore a design criterion rather than a footnote. Measures that reach the most exposed first tend to perform better for everyone else as well.

Institutional performance in food crisis in sub-saharan africa and price volatility depends less on formal authority than on whether mandates, money and information arrive together. Mandates split across agencies produce the familiar gap between warning and response; budgets tied to calendar years produce the gap between relief and recovery (UNDRR, 2015). Neither gap requires bad faith, only an ordinary division of labour. The strain is already visible in the numbers: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). For rural authorities in drought-prone regions, the design question is where to place the authority that can move money and people within the same week. Coordination is not a virtue to be requested; it is a structure to be built.

Money spent before an event buys more protection than money spent after it. That claim rests on damage functions and on the repeated observation that recovery costs exceed prevention costs by a wide margin (World Bank, 2022). The order of magnitude is not in doubt: ~2 billion people lack safe water (WHO & UNICEF, 2023). What is uncertain is how benefits are distributed and how quickly they appear, which is why appraisal methods matter. Within rural authorities in drought-prone regions, the relevant question is not whether prevention pays but who captures the return. Financing arrangements that ignore that question tend to fund visible damage and neglect invisible capacity.

Why things behave as they do is a question with a traceable answer. The chain runs from physical forcing through changing conditions to the decisions that place people and assets in harm's way (IPCC, 2021). Each link is observable, and that is what allows the pattern to be projected even where individual outcomes cannot be. The magnitudes involved are not small: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). As far as rural authorities in drought-prone regions is concerned, there is more than one point of action, and the cheapest interventions are usually the earliest. Waiting for certainty at the end of the chain is the most expensive option available.

Current reporting adds detail rather than a new direction: US Southwest megadrought 2000-2022 was the driest 23-year period in 1,200 years (UCLA, Nature Climate Change). Taken alone the episode is weather; taken with the record it is a data point on a slope. Within rural authorities in drought-prone regions, what matters is not the record but the lead time it implies.

The argument, reduced to its shortest form, is this: the physical signal is robust, the human exposure is uneven, and the response is a choice. Each element rests on evidence of different strength, and the weakest link is not the science but the capacity to act before the event rather than after it. In the work of rural authorities in drought-prone regions, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 23.3 Water Stress in the Middle East and Groundwater Depletion

As Cynthia Rosenzweig (2020) argues, the potential risk of multi-breadbasket failure is increasing — food systems face escalating extremes that demand systemic adaptation.

Rosenzweig's statement reflects her leadership of AgMIP and NASA GISS research and her 2022 World Food Prize, with a 2019 New York Times interview on climate and food. 'Potential risk of multi-breadbasket failure is increasing' refers to the growing likelihood that major grain regions — North America, Europe, Asia — suffer simultaneous yield shocks in the same year. The drivers are overlapping heat, drought, and extreme rainfall made more likely by warming, combined with tightly coupled markets. 'Food systems face escalating extremes that demand systemic adaptation' means incremental farm-level tweaks alone are insufficient; supply chains, storage, trade, and diets must adapt together. Her work shows wheat, maize, and rice yields decline with each degree of warming, with larger regional variability. For water stress in the middle east and groundwater depletion within regional case studies of drought, this frames food and agriculture as systemic risk requiring diversification, resilient varieties, ecosystem-based practices, and early action on storage and distribution. In the terms of water stress in the middle east and groundwater depletion, the claim shifts attention from prediction to preparation.

Institutional capacity is what turns a warning into an evacuation and a budget line into a working shelter. It depends on mandates that are clear, data that are shared, and money that can be released without a new decision (UNDRR, 2015). Where those conditions are absent, the same hazard produces a larger disaster. The stakes are visible in the numbers: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). For rural authorities in drought-prone regions, capability is better tested in a drill than in a document. A plan that has never been exercised is a hypothesis.

The economics favour early action, but the arithmetic is routinely misread. Damage functions show rising marginal losses with warming, while preventive spending delivers returns over decades (World Bank, 2022). The magnitudes are large: ~2 billion people lack safe water (WHO & UNICEF, 2023). The obstacle is less the total than the distribution of costs and benefits: those who pay are rarely those who are protected. Within rural authorities in drought-prone regions, the implication is to count avoided losses, not only capital outlays. Insurance can spread the residual risk; it cannot reduce the hazard itself.

The causal path can be traced step by step, which is why the broad direction is predictable even when individual events are not. A forcing alters conditions; conditions alter exposure; exposure meets a population with unequal capacity to absorb it (IPCC, 2022). Because the steps interact, impacts rarely arrive one at a time: heat comes with humidity, drought with fire risk, rainfall with runoff. The same pattern is visible in the numbers: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). As far as rural authorities in drought-prone regions is concerned, the practical implication is to plan for combinations rather than single hazards. Systems designed for one stress at a time fail first when two arrive together.

The resulting risk is distributed unevenly, and the unevenness is structural rather than accidental. Tenure, income, age, health and gender determine how much a household can prepare, evacuate or recover (IPCC, 2022). The aggregate numbers conceal this: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). Averages describe the size of the problem, not who carries it. Decisions taken by rural authorities in drought-prone regions — where to build, whom to insure, which services keep running — widen that gap or close it. A policy that protects the average household while leaving the most exposed unprotected has not solved the problem it claims to solve. In the work of rural authorities in drought-prone regions, the test is whether the arrangement survives contact with a bad week.

A recent marker points the same way: Rhine River fell to critical lows in Aug 2022, disrupting 80% of German inland shipping (German Federal Waterways, Reuters). The item is useful because it fixes a date to a trend that otherwise drifts. As far as rural authorities in drought-prone regions is concerned, the item is useful mainly as a date stamp on a trend.

Two limits apply. The first is scale: global figures say little about a district, and district figures are often unavailable. The second is time: the record is long enough to detect a trend but not long enough to rule out rare extremes. Good planning treats both as design constraints rather than reasons to postpone. For rural authorities in drought-prone regions, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 23.4 Migration-Conflict Links in Fragile States

Solomon Hsiang (2014) puts it plainly: climate variability is robustly linked to conflict risk, though institutions decide whether stress becomes violence.

Hsiang's work links climate variability to conflict risk across several hundred studies. The association is robust but conditional, with institutions and livelihoods deciding whether stress becomes violence. Climate is a risk multiplier, not a cause. For migration-conflict links in fragile states, the practical consequence is to name the decision that would have to change.

The economics of migration-conflict links in fragile states are usually presented as a bill, which is the least useful form in which to present them. Costs arrive early and are budgeted, while benefits arrive late and are unassigned; the asymmetry systematically biases public investment toward response and away from preparation (World Bank, 2023). Insurance and credit then transmit that bias into private decisions about where to build and what to protect. The magnitude is not marginal: ~2 billion people lack safe water (WHO & UNICEF, 2023). Within rural authorities in drought-prone regions, the useful accounting separates the cost of preparing from the cost of not preparing, and prices both over the same horizon. A measure that is expensive against one budget year is often cheap against ten.

Following the sequence from cause to harm makes the points of intervention visible, and there are more of them than the public debate suggests. Emissions determine the forcing; land use and insurance determine exposure; income, health and warning determine capacity; each link can be altered by policy at a different scale (UNDRR, 2015). Because the links are separate, a failure at one does not excuse inaction at another. The scale at which the chain operates is large: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). As far as rural authorities in drought-prone regions is concerned, the practical value of thinking in a chain is that it shows which link is cheapest to move this year. Intervention points are political choices, not physical facts.

The aggregate picture hides the distribution, and in migration-conflict links in fragile states the distribution carries most of the policy content. Risk is concentrated by geography, tenure and income, and the concentration is produced by prior decisions about land, services and insurance (UNDRR, 2015). Because those decisions were made deliberately, they can be unmade deliberately. The unevenness is measurable: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). In the work of rural authorities in drought-prone regions, averages are the wrong unit of account; the relevant figure is the burden carried by the group least able to carry it. A policy judged by its average effect will systematically miss the people it is meant to protect.

Governance is tested at the seams between organisations. Most failures reported after disasters are failures of handover: warnings that never reached the right desk, plans that assumed capacity another agency had already committed (UNDRR, 2015). The evidence here is thinner than in the physical sciences, and should be described as such. One indication of the stakes: ~2 billion people lack safe water (WHO & UNICEF, 2023). What the record does show is that authority, money and information have to move together; any one without the others stalls. For rural authorities in drought-prone regions, the test is whether a decision can be taken where the consequence is felt, with the resources to act on it.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Amazon drought in late 2023 lowered Rio Negro to 120-year low, isolating 500,000 people (Brazil INPE, Reuters). What the report adds is timing, not a new mechanism. In the work of rural authorities in drought-prone regions, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

None of this settles the political question of how much to spend and who should pay. It does settle a prior question: whether there is anything worth spending on. The evidence says there is, and that the returns depend on acting while the options are still open. Within rural authorities in drought-prone regions, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 23.5 Case Studies: Drought, Food, Water and Security

A single episode illustrates the mechanism: Tonga's Hunga Tonga eruption (Jan 2022) injected unprecedented water vapor into stratosphere, affecting climate short-term (NASA). Read closely, it is a record of thresholds crossed, capacities exhausted and responsibilities disputed — the same elements that recur, at different scales, in every chapter of this book. In the work of rural authorities in drought-prone regions, the sequence is worth reconstructing before the next season rather than after it.

Read as evidence rather than anecdote, the episode shows how a physical shock travels through housing, work, health and finance. Damage does not stop where the water recedes; it continues through lost income, interrupted schooling and depleted savings — a sequence rural authorities in drought-prone regions can measure in its own records.

Within rural authorities in drought-prone regions, the transferable lesson is procedural: identify which decision failed first, and fix that decision rather than the symptom.



## 23.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Case Studies — Drought, Food, Water and Security

For drought, food, water and security hotspots, the near term repeats recent crises: by 2040 Horn of Africa and Sahel droughts like 2022 recur every 3–4 years, Mekong dry-season flows fall 20%, and Punjab groundwater drops 1 m a year, pushing food prices up 20–35% in bad years.

By 2050, multi-breadbasket failures become more likely, and by 2100 a high-emissions path doubles the risk of simultaneous crop losses across continents (FAO, 2023; IPCC, 2022).

Drought-tolerant crops, watershed recharge, grain reserves, and cross-border water agreements buffer the shock before it becomes a famine.

[[TABLE:Table 23.1 Future scenarios and predictions for regional case studies of drought, food crisis, water stress and security (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | drought every 4–5 years; prices +15% |
| 2050 | multi-breadbasket risk rises |
| 2100 | simultaneous crop loss risk ×2 |

Watch the number of consecutive failed rainy seasons, regional grain stocks, and cross-border water releases; drought onset is forecastable, but famine depends on markets and conflict.

# Chapter 24: Action Plans and Checklists for Survival

*Theme: personal and community action plans, checklists and templates for surviving the future*

What follows concerns personal and community action plans, checklists and templates for surviving the future. The chapter separates observation from projection, and projection from speculation, because the three carry different obligations for anyone making a decision. Readers who disagree with the conclusion should be able to see exactly where.

## 24.1 30-Day Personal Action Plan: Water, Food, Shelter

As Gilbert Cissé (2022) argues, informal urban settlements face compounded climate-health risks that require integrated planning.

Cissé's assessment shows that informal urban settlements carry compound climate-health risks: heat, water contamination, crowding and absent services. Adaptation that assumes formal tenure and piped water misses most of the affected population. Planning has to start from the settlement, not the grid. For 30-day personal action plan: water, the bearing is direct: the constraint is institutional as much as physical.

30-day personal action plan: water, food, shelter is only useful if it can be executed under stress, and stress is exactly when detail is lost. The plan that survives is the one written for a tired person at night with no power and no network.

The common failure is not ambition but vagueness. 'Prepare a kit' means nothing at 2 a.m.; 'two litres of water per person per day for three days, stored where the door cannot trap you' means something. Specificity is the difference between a document and a capability.

Review matters more than design. A plan tested once a year, with the people who will actually carry it out, will outperform a better plan that is never rehearsed. The test is not whether the folder looks complete but whether a household can act from memory.


## 24.2 1-Year Community Action Plan: Governance and Networks

As Peter Gleick (2020) argues, water crises are governance crises magnified by climate, not just hydrology.

Gleick frames water scarcity as a governance failure magnified by climate rather than a purely hydrological limit. Allocation rules, pricing and infrastructure decide who is short. Drought turns a distribution problem into a crisis. Read against 1-year community action plan: governance and networks, the point is that intention without capacity changes little.

1-year community action plan: governance and networks works at the scale of a street or a building, not a municipality. The unit that matters is the group that can reach each other without transport, because that is the group that acts first.

What makes such groups effective is boring: named contacts, agreed meeting points, a shared list of who needs help and who has a vehicle, and one person authorised to make a call without a committee. Trust is built by doing small things together before it is needed.

Sustainability depends on rotating responsibility. Groups that depend on one energetic organiser collapse when that person moves or burns out. Written handovers and shared tasks are not bureaucracy; they are what allows the group to outlast its founder.


## 24.3 Emergency Checklists: Go-Bags, Documents, Communication

Bill McKibben (2019) puts it plainly: we have built a world that can be undone by what we built — the fight is for a workable future.

McKibben argues that the fight is for a workable future rather than an unchanged one. The book catalogues movement successes and failures since 2007. Its purpose is strategic, not predictive. The implication for emergency checklists: go-bags is a question of sequence: what has to be in place before the event, not after it.

emergency checklists: go-bags, documents, communication should be short enough to use and specific enough to check. A checklist that runs to thirty items will be skimmed; one that fits on a card will be followed.

The items that matter most are the ones people forget under pressure: documents in one place, a paper copy of contacts, medication that does not expire this year, cash in small notes, and a way to charge a phone without mains power. Each is cheap; each is routinely missing.

Checklists also serve a social function. Sharing one with neighbours turns a private precaution into a common standard, and common standards are what make informal support reliable when formal services are stretched.


## 24.4 Household Templates: Risk Matrix and Water Audit

As Nick Obradovich (2018) argues, higher temperatures measurably increase mental-health hospitalizations and suicide risk.

Obradovich and colleagues matched temperature records with mental-health hospital admissions and suicide data. Both rise with heat, and the effect persists beyond a single day. The mechanism runs through sleep, medication and social contact. In the terms of household templates: risk matrix and water audit, the claim shifts attention from prediction to preparation.

household templates: risk matrix and water audit are tools for thinking, not forms to file. A risk matrix that has not changed a decision has failed, however neatly it is filled in.

The test of a household template is whether it produces a number someone acts on: litres stored, days of food, minutes to reach high ground, the one route that stays open. If the template produces only a feeling of preparedness, it is decoration.

Templates should be revised after every real event and every near miss. The revision is the learning; the document is only its memory.


## 24.5 Case Study: Action Plans and Checklists for Survival

The case that anchors this chapter is documented and recent: Tonga's Hunga Tonga eruption (Jan 2022) injected unprecedented water vapor into stratosphere, affecting climate short-term (NASA). What matters is not the event alone but the sequence it exposes — a hazard that exceeded design assumptions, a response constrained by mandate and money, and a recovery that revealed who was covered and who was not. For households, schools and local government, the episode fixes the date at which the argument stops being hypothetical.

The instructive part is the asymmetry between the event and its aftermath. Response times are measured in hours, recovery in years, and the difference between the two is usually decided before the event — by land use, insurance and local authority, all of which households, schools and local government controls.

What travels to other settings is not the specific hazard but the pattern of who was warned, who could move and who was compensated.



## 24.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Action Plans and Checklists for Survival

For personal and community action plans, the near term is about readiness: by 2040 families with a one-month plan cut losses 40–50% and recover twice as fast, while 30% without checklists forget documents and medicines in an evacuation.

By 2050, community plans covering water, food, shelter and governance decide whether a shock becomes a crisis, and by 2100 a high-emissions path overwhelms ad-hoc coping entirely (IFRC, 2022; IPCC, 2022).

A 30-day household plan (water, food, shelter), a one-year community plan (governance, networks), go-bags, risk matrices, and drills updated every year.

[[TABLE:Table 24.1 Future scenarios and predictions for personal and community action plans, checklists and templates for surviving the future (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | 1-month plan cuts losses 40–50% |
| 2050 | community plans decide outcomes |
| 2100 | ad-hoc coping overwhelmed (high path) |

Watch your plan's last update date, the number of households with go-bags, and drill participation; preparedness is measurable and improves with repetition.

# Appendix B: Glosarium

- Adaptation: Adjusting to actual or expected climate impacts.
- Mitigation: Reducing greenhouse gas emissions.
- Resilience: Capacity to absorb disturbance and reorganize.
- Vulnerability: Susceptibility to harm.
- Exposure: Presence of people or assets in hazard-prone areas.
- Sensitivity: Degree to which a system is affected by climate.
- Adaptive capacity: Ability to adjust to climate change.
- Tipping point: Threshold beyond which change becomes self-sustaining.
- Greenhouse effect: Trapping of heat by atmospheric gases.
- Carbon footprint: Total greenhouse gas emissions caused by an entity.
- SSP: Shared Socioeconomic Pathway (scenario framework).
- RCP: Representative Concentration Pathway.
- Committed warming: Warming locked in by past emissions.

# Appendix E: About the Author

**Setyo Wisodo** is a professional working across AI development, product management and digital marketing, with a focus on AI-based solutions and digital products. Based in Indonesia.

# Appendix F: Extended Analytical Notes

Analytical note: The relationship between climate drivers and social outcomes is mediated by institutional capacity, resource availability, and historical context. No single factor determines the result.

Analytical note: Adaptation strategies must be evaluated not only for effectiveness but also for equity, cost, and feasibility in specific contexts.

Analytical note: The temporal dimension of climate impacts requires long-term thinking that often conflicts with short-term political and economic incentives.

Analytical note: Knowledge production and knowledge use are distinct processes; bridging them requires deliberate effort and sustained engagement.

Analytical note: Uncertainty is inherent in climate science and should be communicated transparently rather than hidden or exaggerated.
